- **Ch.12 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; "The Kirrha Road". SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20 (polish cycle 1 + F1–F2; continuity
  W-21, W-22): every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** **The fight, fast and unglamorous, mostly unseen:** before it,
  to the stranger, "You want somebody else." and to Mara, "Go on up to the boy,"; "The hand stayed at Valens's side."; "I saw Valens's
  hand shut on the wrist above it."; the stick "came down across Valens's shoulders"; he and the talker "pitched off the edge of the
  road together, into the ditch below the terrace"; "Something struck stone down there, twice."; "Valens climbed out with the stick
  coming down at him. It landed on his forearm, and he kept coming, and the big man dropped it and ran."; "There was a knife in his
  hand, and it was not his own." **His first use of her name is a warning, untagged, from the ditch: "Mara."** (never a callback; do
  not stage a second "first time"). **Practical after:** "Are you cut?" / "He looked for blood on me regardless."; he cuts the dead
  man's purse ("When Valens climbed back up he had a purse on a cut thong. He had left the man everything else."); "Get the string on
  to the ford," (to Felix); "This is the god's road." … "Let the god's people find him." / "That's what it was. A traveler with his
  purse cut." (he authors the finders' story); the cracked jar: he cut the ropes "and the jar came off into his arms, and he heaved it
  over the terrace edge, down among the olives", then "picked a sliver of pot out of the hair there and threw it after the man"; he
  reads the walkers far below and moves the string ("Now,"); at the ford, "Valens chose a stone from the bank, near a jar's weight, and
  roped it opposite the single one on the mule that had lost hers." (no knot, no hands read as a mood).
- **Voice under pressure (first shown on the page, Ch.12):** his speech does not change register under pressure. In the fight he says
  one word, "Mara.", untagged, from the ditch. Straight after it his lines are as short and practical as on any working day ("Are you
  cut?" / "Get the string on to the ford," / "Now,"). The pressure goes into his body instead, and only once the work is done, at the
  fall: "They were shaking." / "It started in his shoulders and ran out along his arms, and the water jumped off his fingers with it."
  / "the thumb kept slipping off the nail" / "He gave that up and held his hands still under the water, and they would not be still." /
  "He was breathing through his mouth." He turns his shoulder to hide the hands, "and I saw them anyway". **Mara steadies him (the
  emotional surprise: his, not hers):** she takes his wrists and presses them flat to the stone under the fall, and does not speak; "It
  stopped by degrees." / "He let his forehead come to rest on my hair. He stayed there." / "His stayed flat on the stone. He took them
  down himself." He says nothing during it and nothing about it afterward. His next line is about the camera ("Is it broken?"), and the
  one after that begins the telling, in the same flat witness's Greek as his road talk. Under pressure he gets plainer, never louder
  and never figurative. **The shaking is a one-time body rebel, SPENT as an event:** no later chapter gives him trembling hands as his
  pressure sign (that would be a signature gesture; Ch.23's "he is the one comforted" finds another body and sense). **Injuries carried
  into Ch.13 (three days on; do not show him unmarked):** "There was a graze along his cheekbone with grit in it." (he "plainly knew
  nothing about it"); "Across the left one a welt stood up where the stick had caught it, red and raised, and the knuckles of that hand
  were split."; the stick across the shoulders.
- **Ch.12, the rest of the page:** **Onion memory (first surfacing), triggered:** Mara treads on a clump of flat leaves at the pool
  ("they smelled green and sharp under my heel"): "Onions," … "They hung them under the eaves. In strings, to dry." Never explained.
  **Trust acts:** "He untied the pouch from his belt and put it into my hands. Through the leather the watch was going."; returned at
  the ford: "At the stepping stones I gave him back his pouch. He put the purse in my hand." (no look inside, no line). He washes the
  stranger's knife after the closeness ("running his thumb along the groove to clear it"). **The camera:** at the stream he asks only
  "Is it broken?" (a working thing, his axis). **The telling (his longest speech so far; a witness's chronology, no grievance):** "They
  gave me the wooden sword at Corinth," / "He had the year right, the one on the road." / "The Gaul was older than me, and slow on his
  left side. Nobody on the benches knew that. The emperor never got out of his chair." / "The lanista had a copy made for me on a
  tablet. I kept it in a box." / "After the sword I needed work. Celer was legate then, on the proconsul's staff." / "I swore to her in
  his atrium, in front of his household gods." / "When he was away I slept across her door. Nobody laid a hand on her in two years. I
  was a good bodyguard. That was the use of me." / "He'd bought a house he couldn't pay for, out where the cypresses are." / "He was
  sorry about it. He said so, more than once, with her standing there. She didn't look at me." / "One was the girl who did her hair,
  and she cried all through it. They'd been shown where to stand." / "Put a wife away for that, and you keep a share of what she
  brought. Her father had given plenty with her." / "by the fishpond, where the house could watch" / "his steward went through my box
  and took the tablet" / "The magistrate's book is still in the record office at Corinth, with my name in it." / "I was over his garden
  wall the same night. A fisherman took me across the gulf for my belt buckle." / "They asked my name and I gave them Valens." / the
  mules "off a Corinthian dealer who couldn't feed them till spring" / "Her brother took her home," … "Celer kept the boy."
- **The legal claim as the page now has it (continuity W-21; the prose wins over foundation §3):** the freedom WAS registered: "I put
  the Gaul down, and he sent the sword out to me across the sand, and a magistrate wrote my name in his book." The registration is not
  in dispute. Celer's claim is a prior sale: "His story was that he'd bought me from the lanista the month before the games, so the
  lanista had no right to free me, and the magistrate had written down a mistake. So I was his." In plot terms Celer's case rests on a
  backdated bill of sale. The words "bill of sale" and "backdated" are NOT on the page; Valens says only "His story was". **Consequence
  for Ch.15, 17 and 22:** the Corinth record proves the lanista freed him before witnesses and the emperor, but on its own it no longer
  refutes Celer. The Rome suit has to defeat the sale itself: the lanista's books, the date, or the fact that Celer hired him for wages
  after the games ("After the sword I needed work. Celer" … "wanted a man to walk behind his wife"). Valens never argues the law; he
  gives the chronology and stops. (Foundation §3's "never registered before a magistrate" and the Ch.17/Ch.22 outline beats still carry
  the old claim: flagged for the architect/orchestrator, not patched in this file.)
- **Ch.12, knowledge path, Felix, plans:** **Knowledge path (one passage):** "Last autumn a freedman of her brother's found me at the
  harbor," … "The brother's gone to law for her dowry. He wanted me to come and stand up and tell the court it was a lie. I said I
  wouldn't. He'll have told them where he found me. Inside the month that notice went up in Corinth. You saw the month on it." / "I saw
  it too." (it stands alone) / "Five hundred for me walking," … "Whoever wants five hundred brings a rope. Those three brought none. He
  doesn't want me back. He wants me in a ditch." He gives NO reason for refusing the freedman (evasion: fear). How Celer knew the
  harbor is Valens's own inference ("He'll have told them where he found me."). **With Felix:** the crust lie is Felix's own ("It was
  that crust," … "It was off."); Valens answers "Don't give the goat any more of it," and gives the cover story ("If anybody in the
  town asks, we came on a man in the ditch below the ford, and the road had been at him before us.") / "You came on a man in a ditch."
  / "Tell it anyway." / to "He believed me": "He'd settled on robbers back on the road," / to "Did he go down straight off?": "No."
  (his ONE bare "No." in Ch.12; the Ch.11 lie answered truly, without "one blow"). **With the graybeard:** "There's thorn all along
  this road," **Plans, stated, no request:** "Three days," / "A knife in the precinct would have a hundred men on it. They'll want me
  on a road, not in a crowd." / "The goat comes to the yard the evening before." / "When it's over, the mules are for the high
  grazing," … "Above the cave. Up there you see a man coming a whole morning off. I'll stay up the summer." (he does not ask her to
  come) / Kallia: "She hears what the donkey man heard." … "Kallia, then. Nobody else." **Names for him on the page:** the stranger's
  "Lupus" and "*Rudiarius*". Nobody calls him "mule-man" in Ch.12 (the word is in Ch.6 and Ch.11 only); the graybeard says "You've been
  in the thorns." **Page counts (FINAL):** no simile in his speech; bare "No." 1; bare "Felix." 0; walk-offs after a line 2 ("Now," and
  the slap on the hindmost mule; "He was first down the goat path to the ford."); narrated non-answers 0; stopped-work tell 0; a knot
  near his hands 0; boot lace 0 (cut, continuity W-05). **Spent in Ch.12:** the shaking hands held still at the fall; forehead on her
  hair; the pouch handed over to hold; "Is it broken?"; the purse cut to stage a robbery; "Let the god's people find him."; "The
  emperor never got out of his chair."; "That was the use of me." (the at-risk single use); "Whoever wants five hundred brings a
  rope."; "He wants me in a ditch."; "There's thorn all along this road,"; "He'd settled on robbers back on the road,"; "Tell it
  anyway."; the slept-across-her-door detail (said once; Ch.14's doorway is staged without pointing at it); a stone roped to balance a
  load.

- **Ch.13 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20, polish cycle 1 + FZ1–FZ3; every quote below is on the page,

  that was purple at its heart and yellow toward the elbow, and that hand's knuckles had scabbed over, all four." / shoulders held "all of a
  piece", turning "his whole body round, hips and all" (the stick). In a laurel wreath "like something another man had put there while he
  slept". **The pills refused by his hands:** "He did not touch the two small white things in my palm." / "After," … "I want my head, in
  there." **Attention, not fear:** "Not yet." / "If you see the one you hit, send the boy to me in the line. Stay among the women." / "The
  boy does as he's told." (Felix later vaults the wall after the bandaged boy). At the altar "Valens kept his grip on the horns until it was
  over." **The inside, as a bodyguard saw it** (told in Kallia's court "with a bowl of her lentils going cold on his knee"): the door and
  the names ("Praxias son of Kleon, of Delphi, for the house of Kallia. He gave it out twice, the second for the Thessalians' benefit.");
  **mind-reading, wrong in a mundane way:** the short-sighted Euthydikos "put his nose a hand from mine to make me out. He was slow about
  it, and he knew the face." (Mara: "He puts his nose that close to everything,"); **the carrier's name: "I gave him Felix."** (CH11 open
  item: shown without a gloss; the priest's wax reads "Maira, of Syracuse, of the house of Kallia, a kinswoman"); the porch writing ("Big.
  Men stop at it and move their lips."; he pretends nothing, Mara answers Felix, and there is no gaze beat); smoke, laurel and barley on the
  hearth; "The floor slopes to the back, not much, but a man with bad feet would feel it."; "There's one door. I looked for another."; the
  bench, the rods, the praying Boeotian, the iron chair ("He said a chair was a chair."); the stone: "Stone color," … "and it came to my
  waist." … "A man was posted beside it watching the bench from start to finish, and I didn't care for him, so I watched him." … "He had a
  cough."; the screen, where the priest "reads the question off the wax, at the screen, in an ordinary voice"; the voice described, never
  quoted ("a woman, not loud"; the break; "Hoarse. Higher than I'd have put her." / "Tired."); **a word not Greek, twice, which he did not
  keep** ("I didn't keep it. It wasn't in what he gave me." / "It's gone,"); the priest's raised flat hand, shown with his own scarred hand;
  "Hers were fewer; that much was plain. He listened to all of hers, and after it he gave me his."; **the question he would not put:** "I
  told him the goat was hers." and, of Praxias in the porch, "I told him it was hers." **Out of the temple:** "Not here." / "At Kallia's."
  (his one walk-off), then silent "the length of the Way". **The anchor:** stands unasked in the middle of the court, feet set, weight even,
  "his hands open at his sides, and fixed on the wall above the woodstack", and gives the answer once, word for word, "in a voice that was
  new to me"; asked for the mountain part alone, he cannot start in the middle ("his hand lifted an inch from his knee"; "A trainer at the
  school called the forms. Eight or ten cuts at a breath" … "Get one wrong and you started again from the first. You don't go in at the
  fourth." / "I can't take it from the sea onward. I'd have to start at the beginning."). **The priest's gloss, passed on unjudged:** "He
  said that was a fine answer for a Syracusan and I was to tell her so. I'm telling you." **Questions about her country:** "Is your country
  west?" / "Where do they put the dead, there?" / "Inside the walls?" / "Where does the town stop, then?" (answered: "Where a board by the
  road says it does,"). **To the riddle:** "It's what he said." / "Then it's something." / "You'll try the cave again," / "It's shut." **To
  Felix's Capua:** no word; "Valens rose and took his bowl to the lean-to, and put it in Kallia's hands." / "The string's had no water since
  daybreak," (Felix does not hear "the school"). "The boy wanted it written down somewhere." / "Mine's written. At Corinth." (CH12-8 NOT

  (her "There's floor," closes it; Ch.14 shows him across the doorway). **Watches the street:** "There he set his shoulders to the wall with

  **bare "No." 1** (to "Did the priest give you anything about when?"); narrated non-answers 0; walk-offs 1; no watch (none until Ch.18); no
  knot near his hands. **Spent in Ch.13:** the wreath as if put there while he slept; nose-to-nose mind-reading; "I gave him Felix."; "Stone

  "She kept you." (Mara: "Theano's jar was empty."); "He gave the crows' house above us one look."; walks "on the house side, half a step
  behind". **Asleep across the storeroom door** on the blanket Kallia laid ("crosswise on the threshold"), in his cloak, facing out, "the

  up on one elbow. He had moved in silence." / "The knife from the road was out of his belt and lying back along his forearm, point to the
  elbow. He was facing the court and the gate and the black beneath the gallery. He spoke under the sound of the Thessalians' breathing." /
  "Was that you?" / "He left the knife where it was." (FZ2: the knife stays out through the question) / after the well story, "The knife
  went back into his belt." / "The moon's down by midnight," … "She'll dig it crooked." (practical, the sky and the ground, an unintended
  joke; no comparison; the moon agrees with the 7th of Bysios, continuity C-01) / "He lay down again, facing out, and pulled the cloak up
  over his ear. He slept almost at once." **Deliberation pause for Valens: 0** (the draft's weighing beat is cut). **Gaze-holding frame: 0**
  (FZ2 cut the one C2 had brought in). Mara's narration of what he would ask if told ("where it was and how far, and whether the road was
  good, and how the ground lay round it") is his attention axis seen from outside. When she steps over him he "slept on, or let me think he
  did". **Before light he has gone down for the mules**, leaving word with Kallia (Ch.13's "You'll try the cave again" honored as an act).
  Bare "No." 0; narrated non-answers 0; walk-offs 0 on the page; no knot; no watch. **Spent in Ch.14:** the knife along the forearm on
  waking; the knife held through a question and put back after the answer ("He left the knife where it was."); "The moon's down by
  midnight,"; "She'll dig it crooked."; the cloak over the ear.
- **Ch.15 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; the high pasture, July AD 71; his longest talking day before Ch.34. SYNCED 2026-10-20: FINAL
  page, polish cycle 1 + the CH15-1 spring line + Z1–Z6; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):**
  - **Lines (each once):**
    - the dawn and the fig: "Cool. She'll do the troughs."; to Mnason, "Cut your own." / "The gorge, then." / "It would."; "He'll be
      over those stones some night." (his mind-reading, aimed at Mnason); to Felix, "Eat one and find out.";
    - noon: "The light in the cave,"; "It's not warm,"; "When it's used up?"; "Don't burn it, then,"; "You'd no salt on you."; "Titus
      has just burned it."; "I know May."; then "Do they still have mules?" and the practical questions that follow it ("Who leads
      them?"; "Who's emperor?"; "So the legions went home."; "The roads. Does anybody mend them?"; "Where's the god gone?"; "The
      spring?"; "Is the sea still salt?"; "Who's looking for you, there?"; "That's true, then.");
    - the camera and the town: "The heavy one," … "Felix's silver."; "It makes everything small,"; "He'll be wanting it, then.";
      "It was made somewhere."; "The god gave you a mountain and a tomb."; "I've heard of Neapolis. Puteoli."; the dust map ("Here's
      the gulf, and here's the heel of Italy"); "With the light, and the box you look through."; "How will you know the door, among
      all those tombs?";
    - the bargain: "My paper," … "is in Corinth. The book with my name in it."; "Hold your breath,"; "I'll want that book in my hand
      before I set foot in Italy."; "So's the brother. Her brother."; "The freedman told me which house."; "That was last autumn.";
      "Half Corinth sat on those benches. They've not all died."; "She'll be in it."; "In that court I'll have the brother's men on the
      benches round me," / "Celer's men chose a road for it."; "As man and wife."; "That's for the shipmaster, at the gangway."; "He
      asks it before he asks the fare,"; "Delphi thinks you're on a boat. Let it."; "Kallia will tell him you sailed."; "There's the
      silver from the ford."; "mules hardened on a mountain"; "He comes."; "Why?" (of her plan to go home); **"I'll wait outside it,"**;
    - the afternoon and supper: "Italy's long,"; "Nobody'd give me anything for the black."; "I saw the juniper,"; "It's good,";
      "He's called me that since our first night."; "He never asked."; "They walk alongside,"; at the fire, "I believe that cave had a
      light in it with no flame. I was there to see it." / "The rest is yours." / "It'll get you on a boat." / "Then I'll believe it,";
    - the hut: "The boy,"; "You wear it to bed."; "That's the fork,"; "I was watching the figs."; "Here?"; "Was it the pole?"; "He can
      have the end that's standing."; "do they marry at the gangway?"; "Here you say it before witnesses, and then everybody eats.
      Corinth has clerks."
  - **His disbelief, by act:** "He had heard all of it and put it away as Kallia put away a lodger's tale of a sister who married a
    magistrate." He believes only the flameless light.
  - **His hands, a different object each time:** the black's near fore (once, at dawn); the figs, touched "with a fingertip"; the
    knife, shaving "by feel, with no glass"; the flashlight, held "flat on his palm"; the camera ("the wrong end to his eye first");
    a thread of thyme; his boot heel in the dust; splitting pine "round the side of the hut" (out of sight, so Felix's flinch goes
    unwatched); a root of juniper laid on the embers; the Pentax strap, "two-handed", hung "with the lens turned to the wall". **He
    walks the string's line at dusk "as he did every night"** (one habitual; see the WATCH in the at-risk block).
  - **In the hut:** the head on the roof poles, "He told the roof something in Latin"; "His palm was under my skull before the floor
    was"; the skinned knuckles; "His knuckles stayed at my mouth, and he was quite still."; "he held still for me through it, as he
    could hold still for anything; then the stillness left him, the whole of it at once"; "His jaw had roughened again since noon";
    "his cheek on my shoulder"; "His arm went heavy."
  - **Rules kept:** no comparison in his speech; "Why?" only of a plan, never of a feeling; one general-truth saying ("Here you say it

- **Ch.16 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; Corinth winter AD 71–72 and the autumn crossing. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21:
  dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity repair, polish cycle 1 + F1–F8; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):**
  - **CH12-8 SETTLED on the page: "Valens" is his own name.** Plautia, who heard him sworn as her bodyguard, has always called him
    Valens ("You cannot have the book, Valens."; "There is a stool, Valens,"); the benches knew him as **Lupus** ("tell each other they
    have seen Lupus"). The leaf in the magistrate's book reads "Gaius Iulius Valens, freedman of Gaius"; the nomen is the lanista's:
    "The lanista's," … "I had it with the sword." / "It was new," (to Plautia's "You said the Gaius quickly, in case somebody should
    take it back."). He gives the brother's porter the name as well: "Valens. He sent his freedman to Kirrha for me." He gave the Kirrha
    quay his real name (Ch.12). The name Ch.22 calls his own is Gaius Iulius Valens.
  - **The watching (Mara's jealousy answered without a question):** at the porch "He let the sack slide to the paving, and before it
    had settled he had looked the street over both ways, slowly. He stood aside against the house wall, a little to the right of the
    step, facing the turning." Plautia waits on the step "until he had looked, and until he had moved", passes him on his left, and at
    the turning "moved her head, not far, to find him". Then "He watched the empty turning a while, then climbed the step, and saw me."
    He looks at Mara's face ("Behind him the street might have been a wall."). **In C4 he stands just inside the door and has not sat
    in that room** ("There is a stool, Valens," … "I'm well here.", answered twice in one narrated sentence). **Back to the wall in
    Ch.16: 0** (the porch beat is "stood aside against the house wall"; the C4 jamb clause is gone). Book-wide WATCH: 2 (Ch.13 and the
    Ch.16 porch).
  - **Lines (each once), by scene:**
    - the porch: "Your feet are wet." / "She gave you that." / "She's done it before. Give it to her woman tomorrow, early, and say
      nothing to her about it. She'll be sorry by dark." / "Wear it, then," / "Take the sacks round. Fill the dog's bowl; it's foul." /
      "There's only the one." (the amphitheater) / "The sacks," / "He'd want in." / "He'd stay in." / "Near the forum. The brother's
      freedman walked with me." and the record office's two doors ("One onto the colonnade, with a porter on a stool and a slave asleep
      at his feet. One at the back, onto a lane, with a baker's oven built against it and a step down.");
    - **the evasion:** "I went for barley. The porter on the colonnade is the proconsul's. The baker's not." (a deadpan sequence, not a
      list); the baker's change ("gave me back too much" / "I bought another loaf with it.");
    - the boat: "They can't," … "It stays in them." / "Hold the dun's head up. If she goes down on this she'll not get up till
      Lechaion." / "She won't die." / "The light." / "Get round her quarters," … "Don't push. Take the tail when I say, and pull her
      toward you." / "Felix." / to the black, in Latin, "Up, now, you old fool. Up," / "Shut your eyes," (no shown compliance: command
      echo 0) / "How much is left in it?" / "Don't spend it on mules." / "I did." / "It's a long climb," … "for a boy who's been sick
      all day." / "East of the harbor," … "Along there.";
    - the brother's porch: "Valens. He sent his freedman to Kirrha for me." / "My wife," (to the porter);
    - C4: "Which clerk?" / "What goes in the purse?" / "You'll be known," / "I'll be by the baker's oven." / "The baker gives too much
      back." / **bare "No." 1** (Valens said, "No.") / "I'll be at the back door." / "The book," / "I said I'd stand up," … "I'll stand
      up." / "I'm well here." (twice, narrated).
  - **His voice is tagged "low" once** ("Valens's voice in the dark, low."); the second tag was cut.
  - **Rules kept:** no comparison in his speech (the new lines included); bare "No." 1; narrated non-answers 0; walk-offs 1 ("Valens
    went down."); no watch; no knot; no fig opinions (the fig rides "roped to the foot of the mast in its jar", texture only). His
    hands, a different object each time: the barley sack, the loaf ("he took it out and broke it, and handed me half"), a shoulder
    under the black's neck, the halter rope "round his fist", a sack worked "in under her shoulder". He sits once, on the porch bench,

- **Ch.17 as written (Writer, 2026-10-21; the theft, the colonnade, the run. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21: dialogue-polish,
  disruptor, continuity repair, polish cycle 1 + F1–F14; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** **His post, settled on
  the page:** by the baker's oven at the record office's back door, "with a baker's peel in his hands and his forearms white with
  flour, which was the only disguise anybody had been able to think of for him, and it fooled nobody who had ever seen a baker."
  "Valens read my face before he read the case." **He reads the leaf himself** (Mara knows he reads; Felix does not): "He took it by
  its edges, a hand to each, and his thumbs left flour on the wood of the frame." (no wipe) / "His mouth moved on the first word, and
  then it stopped moving, and he read the rest of it with his lips shut, to the end of the witnesses." / "Nine," / "Three of them are
  dead." / "And the book?" / "When?" / "Alone." **Mind-reading, inverted (outline chaos):** he holds the shut leaf "chest-high, in
  plain view of the clerk", watches the clerk's hands, and trusts him: "It's mine." / "Today," / "He'll eat his bread." / "Look at
  him." … "He came out for bread with that man in the colonnade waiting on him. That's a man who's had enough of the day." He is right
  (the clerk says nothing in the colonnade). To the baker: "It's near enough noon." The leaf goes "inside it, flat, against his chest",
  and he "pulled his belt tighter over it"; "He laid his palm on it through the cloth." **He breaks the plan** to stand by her: "Get
  down to Felix," / "Round." / "She's got her face to him, with hired men round her and nobody at her shoulder. He'll talk at her till
  she agrees to something." / "Her bearers are hired men off the Kenchreai road. They'll take his money too, if he offers it." / "The
  ship's the one with the goose carved on her stern. Felix knows it. It goes out on the afternoon breeze, whoever's on it." / "He can
  look at it, and leave her be." (to "He knows your face."). Exit: "He left for the colonnade with the flour still on his arms". At the
  scaffold, no porch protocol and no "at her back": "He had walked in under the planks and stood a pace from her, between her and the
  forum." **Face to face with Celer:** "Legate," / "You hired me," / "And the paper in Rome," / "Her brother's paying my road," … "To
  Rome." (Plautia's Ch.16 question answered in action, in front of her); "Valens stood with his floured hands hanging at his sides."
  After: "You were by yourself." / "That was where he was standing." / "Puteoli. After that, I can't say." **S4:** "Her woman can come
  to Puteoli for it. Walk." / "He's at the Isthmus till dark. Then he goes home and eats. Then he wants his book." / "By then we're a
  day out to sea, wind willing." / the three dead witnesses, one speech: "The doctor at the school. He sewed me up for six years. An
  oil-seller who had the stall by the gate. A trainer who had me from a boy. The other six are strangers to me." / "Keep walking. Don't
  look at him." / "Let him watch it. It's the only road." / to the trainer's "Murmillo," … "Whose school?": "I lead mules." / "Him?" /
  the pine crown: "For Felix," … "He missed the boys." / "He'll ask who won. We'll not know. He can have this." / "I saw where his feet
  stopped." / "That's the breeze," / "Asking him how." / **bare "No." 1** ("Will they wait?" / "No."). **Rules kept:** no comparison in
  his speech; narrated non-answers 0; no reply opens on "Then…"; walk-offs 1 ("Valens went by him."); no fig opinion (the clerk's figs
  are not his subject); no watch; no knot near his hands. His hands: the peel, the leaf by its edges, the belt, the palm on the leaf
  through the cloth, the pine crown, "one hand spread on his chest over the leaf" in the run. **No wipe:** wiping or drying his hands
  before taking an object is CLOSED at 2 (Ch.9, Ch.15). **Spent in Ch.17:** the floured-baker cover; "his thumbs left flour on the wood
  of the frame"; reading his name with his lips shut after the first word; "Three of them are dead."; "It's mine."; "He'll eat his
  bread."; "That's a man who's had enough of the day."; "He'll talk at her till she agrees to something."; "They'll take his money too,
  if he offers it."; "Her brother's paying my road"; "That was where he was standing."; "Her woman can come to Puteoli for it."; "I
  lead mules."; the three dead witnesses (named once; never listed again); "I saw where his feet stopped."; the pine crown bought
  mid-escape; "Asking him how."

- **Ch.18 as written (Writer, 2026-10-21; the Lechaion quay, the same early afternoon. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21 (md5

  the ship's side" and gets "his wind back in long hauls through the nose" (his one breath beat; the draft's second breath, mid-line, is
  cut). **Lines:** "Where's the freedman?" / "Valens. A freedman from the city paid you." (one line, one breath; he does not name
  Plautia's house to a stranger) / "I'll pay the fare again," (the offer stands alone; its doubling tail is cut, continuity W-13) / "You

  nobody's, which is worse." turns it). **The proposal, staged bare:** "Valens faced me with flour dried white in the hair of his

  words: "Will you be my wife, Mara?"**; to her "Yes. Will you?": **"Yes," said Valens** (a bare Yes; bare-No answers 0 this chapter).
  **The warning, then the turn:** "Keep off her heels," Valens called, and the sailor turned to answer him. (the kick's cause is on the
  page, uncommented) / "The boy's," (who pays for the sprats; his one dry line) / "Valens had gone after the black." (F2) before "Cover
  her eyes, Felix. Her eyes." / "Tread on the cleats," … "It gives in the middle." (footing, on the plank with the fig jar). **He never
  names Mara as his wife to the master** (the gatekeeper line is CLOSED after Ch.16); the master talks past Mara to him ("He spoke to
  Valens."; "To Valens he said"). **The watch:** "By then Valens had the thong of his pouch undone. He took out the watch and laid it in
  my hand." No word about it. It is warm from him, floury, running, wound (the crown "took two and stood hard") and near the sun: "The
  hands were at twenty to one, and beside my foot the mooring post laid a short shadow on the stone, tipped a little east." The page
  never says he set it or kept it wound; the Ch.11 fast-reading recall is cut and stays cut. While she reads it: "Valens was minding the
  dun." (C2: upkeep of his animal, not a look-away). He closes the clasp on her RIGHT wrist: "He closed the clasp for me, and his thumb
  was too broad for the catch, so he found its lip with the edge of his nail and pressed. His fingers smelled of bread, and once the
  catch was home he released my wrist." (the catch has no sound). Gives Felix the crown with no speech ("Valens held out the crown.").
  Fetches the black: "Valens brought the black back with a sprat scale stuck to her nose and her flanks dark with sweat". Comes back for
  the fig: "Valens was on the plank again, coming for it, and took the jar's rim on his side". **Rules kept:** no comparison in his
  speech; narrated non-answers 0; no walk-off; no fig opinion (the fig is carried, not discussed); the short narration line
  Valens-was-minding once (the black is "had gone after", F2). **Spent in Ch.18:** the wedding consent and the bare "Yes,"; the proposal
  with the crown in his hand; the watch handed back (the next winding, Ch.24, is shown in fresh words and is not a handing); closing the
  clasp for her; "Tread on the cleats"; "The boy's,"; "I'll pay the fare again,"; the floury watch; the warning, then the turn;
  **minding (or looking away from) an object hand-off: CLOSED at 2 (Ch.13, Ch.18).**

SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21 (md5 `41dedf4d…`): dialogue-polish, hook-craft, disruptor, continuity repair W-01…W-14, polish cycle 1 (L1–L2, C1–C4, R1–R15, D1–D6), finalize F1–F5; quotes checked by script; a CUT entry names a draft string checked to be OFF the

  table" (no seal; the papers work) / **mind-reading, wrong in a mundane way:** "He knew me," / "He walked round his table to look at
  my face." / "He did that to no other man on the mole. He'll know me again." (answered by Mara's "black wax on the end of his

  that comes by sea from Corinth for Valens the muleteer," … "keep it on your board for me." / "A lady's brother." / "Pompeii," … "I'll
  send to you." / a beaker of wine each "that nobody had asked him to buy" / in the loft: "This town of yours." … "Where do the
  beasts drink?" / "Carting? Who wants it?" / "Building what?" / "Ten years," … "They're slow." / "We've a mule with a loose shoe,"
  … "Hold her." / at the salt pans: "Up on the crown with them," … "The edges won't bear a loaded beast." / "Thirty pairs," … "In a
  town this size." / low, at the crossing: "The muleteers have a man, then." (he read the panel himself; Felix still believes he
  cannot read) / "Which gate is nearest?" / "Tomorrow I'm out at that gate after carting, with the string." / "There'll be men
  sleeping in those tombs." / "Go when the road's full," … "and keep to it going and coming." / "It sticks at the sill," / on the roof:
  "They dug the bowl and banked the earth from it round the sides for the seats," … "That saved them stone." (this bowl, past tense,
  ground and footing; the draft's general saying is cut) / "There were," / "How many gates are there?" / "The third?" (to "Seven or
  eight. Three have tombs worth the walk.") / "Some do," / "Out where the dead are. Eat your beans." **Hands, a different object
  each time:** a hand round her ankle on the eighth night ("did not let go till morning"); the black at the hurdle lashing ("he left
  me to see to it"); the leaf; the hipposandal, laced with no race ("Valens had the hoof clamped up on his leg and the lacing
  threaded through its rings; he drew it tight, tried it with his thumb, and set the foot down"); the dun walked through the
  stepping stones "with his other hand on the stone she was eyeing"; the fig on the roof "before he had looked at the bed"; the
  door between the rooms: he "shut it, set the bar in its brackets and lifted it clear, opened it, and closed it once more, slowly,
  to see how it hung on its leather"; the noseband cut "out of the one long strip the nephew had missed" (continuity W-08), tried
  "against his own hand for size". CUT: "before Felix had a hand on her halter". **Stillness:** "as still as the column beside the
  table" under the clerk's stare. **Laugh:** once, "under his breath", at the carter. **The bed:** "the cord at the rotten end let him
  down an inch." **Grata touched his arm; he looked at the place** ("He looked at the place on his sleeve where her hand had been.";
  planted, no line). **No fig opinion** (at the inn he "walked round it once and came away"). **The farm-gate wine:** he "lifted his
  eyes to the vines it had come from, and sent Felix back to the farm gate for another jug." **Knows:** the door is somewhere in the
  tombs (Ch.15); not yet told she found it. **Work:** carting rubble for a contractor at the Stabian Gate, paid by the load, from
  day three. **The leaf:** "The leaf went back under Valens's tunic, with his belt drawn tight over it." **The letter:** word left at
  the Puteoli board. **WATCH (new):** orders to Felix as deflection, 2 ("Hold her." / "Eat your beans."): at most 1 in Ch.20. "Out
  where the dead are" in his mouth: once in the book.

#### AUGUST FENN / "AUGURINUS" — the vanished treasure hunter; b. 1934 Philadelphia; crossed 1968, landed AD 41; Puteoli astrologer (documents Ch.2; in person Ch.25–35; dies winter 78–79)
- **Attention / worldview:** systems and patterns: tides, tremors, star tables, the doors' timing, box
  scores. **He sees people last.** His worldview: his real life is the one he is going back to. He has
  never furnished a room here.
- **Vocabulary band:** **in English, frozen in 1968** (see the pool below), with Navy and treasure-hunter
  bravado and Philadelphia vowels. **In Greek, perfect, formal and courtly** after more than three decades. He
  is the only character whose second language is better than his first is current. His English documents
  move from boyish capitals (1967) to small, tired script (1968).
- **Syntax fingerprint:** **excited:** runaway sentences chained on "and," changing subject mid-stream and
  correcting himself ("No, don't tell me"). **Tired:** four-word fragments. **No enumerating, numbering or
  listing habit, ever** (struck by the no-tics law). His pattern-mind shows in *what he talks about*,
  never in a verbal device.
- **Rhythm:** bursts and collapses; asks questions and refuses the answers.
- **Evasion — never says:** his sister's name in person until Ch.35 (his 1968 notebook names Margaret
  freely; the silence grew over the decades). In Ch.25 "estate sale" tells him she is dead, and he does
  not ask whose; he refuses the notebook until Ch.35. That he is afraid of dying here; the word "home" for
  anywhere in this century. He talks about going back in a confident future tense until the day he dies.
  No deathbed conversion and no wisdom.
- **Physicality / silence:** ink-stained fingers, a tremor in the left hand, a 1968 Zippo turned over and
  over that he refills with lamp oil and cannot make work (it catches once, Ch.35). He pats a crying
  woman very fast. A potted basil.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by vocabulary band (frozen-1968 English against perfect Greek) and
  attention (systems before people)
- **The 1968 idiom pool** (each item **at most twice book-wide** in his speech; draw widely, never lean
  on one): holy cow · for crying out loud · beats me · no dice · aw, nuts · swell · the whole shebang ·
  big shot · on the level · you bet · knock it off · scram · whaddaya know · cripes · I'll be a monkey's
  uncle · *Jesus H. Christ* (once only, Ch.25). Period references, each **at most twice**: Chico Ruiz
  stealing home on 21 September 1964, Gene Mauch, Johnny Callison, Jim Bunning's perfect game, Connie
  Mack Stadium, Horn & Hardart on Chestnut Street, Wanamaker's, *American Bandstand*, Ed Sullivan, LBJ,
  Nixon running, Sputnik.
- **Bleed guard:** Mara must never pick up his idiom, and he must never pick up hers. When he hears a
  1990s word he rages at it; he does not adopt it.
- **Chaos on the page (subjects, never phrases):** **the 1964 Phillies** (Ch.25, 26, 29, 35: the 6½-game
  lead with 12 to play). **The automat** (Ch.25 coconut cream; Ch.27 lemon meringue; Ch.35 he can't
  remember the pie). **Sunk cost** (he finishes every terrible jar of wine he has paid for). **The rage**
  (Ch.26 the "voicemail" letter, once only; never repeated in person, so it cannot become a bit).
- **What he knows (fixes the Ch.25 delivery):** he knows from school that Pompeii dies in 79, and he
  rebuilt his door-tables around it after his 71 and 73 dates failed. He believes the doors are one door
  with many mouths, which is what his "it" means in "the one it brought."
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only voice that runs on 1968 American slang in breathless "and"-chains
  and talks about the future as a place he is going, against Mara's precise 1998 English, which will not
  name the future at all.
- **Ch.2 documents as written (Writer, 2026-10-18):** Thanksgiving 1967 entry (Margaret thinks a scholiast is
  a foot doctor; Father Kostas at St. George's translated the scholion for a roast pork sandwich at the
  Terminal; he slit the uncut pages of an 1838 Leipzig edition at the university library with his thumb,
  up to the note and no further; Margaret made him add the question mark after BIBLIOTHECA "to be
  honest"; "two thousand bucks and a better flashlight"). February 1968: sold the Plymouth to a mailman in
  Jenkintown; TWA to Athens 2 April; Margaret tells the church ladies he is in Mexico. Delphi (room over a
  taverna, a goat at the window); the cold-air entry; "Slept up there. Didn't help."; June "Tired
  today."; last entry dated July 21, 1968 (going in "tomorrow first thing", back before Labor Day).
  Consulate carbon dated 16 Sept 1968: one gray wool overcoat with contents of pockets, forwarded to Miss
  Margaret Fenn. No enumerating anywhere; the handwriting change is carried by Mara's description.
- **Sample line:** "You know what kills me? I had two seats for the Labor Day doubleheader, and some guy
  at Connie Mack sat next to an empty chair all afternoon and never knew it was me, and that was, what,
  a hundred years ago, two thousand, for crying out loud."

---

## MAJOR SECONDARIES

#### KALLIA — widow who keeps a pilgrims' lodging by the Castalian spring, Delphi (Ch.5–16; farewell Ch.16)
- **Attention / worldview:** **costs in labor:** who carries the water, who will clean it, how many
  hands a thing takes. The world is chores and the people who do them.
- **Vocabulary band:** Phocian village Greek; household nouns (jar, broom, wool, hearth, beans, pilgrims,
  rent). Nothing learned and nothing quoted (**no proverbs**: struck).
- **Syntax fingerprint:** short imperatives. **She answers a question with a task** (CAPPED from Ch.10 at **1 chore-reply
  per chapter**, and sending someone off on an errand instead of answering counts as the same device; her other replies
  are silence, an act, a question back or a flat fact: see at-risk after Ch.10 FINAL). Her own questions
  are practical (can you carry water, can you card wool, is she Greek, can she weave), and under them sits
  the question she cannot ask.
- **Rhythm:** few words, then work. She never finishes a speech; she goes and does something instead.
- **Evasion — never says:** her son's name, until the letter forces it once (Ch.10); anything about
  herself or her widowhood; thanks; goodbye (Ch.16 is a sweep and a put-away broom, no speech).
- **Physicality / silence:** **never sits** while anyone else is working. Always something in her hands.
  She touches objects, not people (contrast Grata). The broom laid across the empty room's threshold.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (labor and its cost) and evasion (never speaks of
  herself)
- **Bleed guard:** never notices how a thing is *made* (Mara's); only who has to *do* it.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the woman who replies to every question with a job, against Theano, who
  replies to every question with a complaint about herself.
- **Sample line:** "Water first. Cry after. The jar's by the door and the spring won't come to you."
- **Ch.5 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; first live appearance):** about fifty; broad brown face lined
  across the forehead; gray hair dragged back under a dark kerchief. Comes to the Castalian spring after
  dark with a jar on her head and a lamp in her hand, to fill the jar (the page says only "a lamp", no
  material; it gives no reason beyond the errand).
  Her lodging is a short walk above the spring, up the road and along a lane ("near" the spring, not
  beside the basin). **Her own life before
  she sees Mara:** fishes a pilgrims' limp garland out of the basin and flings it down the slope
  ("Boeotians"), then washes each foot under the overflow with her eyes shut. Lines are imperatives and
  practical questions only: "Out of the basin. People drink that." / "Who beat you?" / "Hands." / "Turn
  them." / "Open your mouth." / "What's that? Lead?" (Mara's amalgam filling) / "Who saw you come down
  here?" / "Can you carry water? … card wool? Weave? Grind? Cook?" / "Whose are you?" / "Bring the jar."
  She **never touches Mara**: she inspects hands and teeth by lamplight and demonstrates opening her own
  mouth and carrying on the head. Answers Mara's long English outburst by pointing at the jar (a task).
  The one sentence about her household, not herself: "I've a houseful of Thebans, and the girl ran off
  at the vintage." On the way home she bangs up a neighbor at night to reclaim her fine horsehair sieve
  (lent "a month" ago for the neighbor's wife to strain her must; it comes back with a hole; she threatens to shame the wife at the spring).
  Mara answers her in English throughout, or by a head-shake (the Greek refusal holds from the
  guide's mimicry on); Kallia reads tone, not words ("Mine." is said in English). Her name reaches Mara only when a pilgrim shouts it from the gallery; to "Who's that you've got?" she
  says "A pair of hands" and, to "Can she sing?", "She can carry." Work orders: ashes out before light,
  hearth swept, two jars from the spring before the pilgrims wake, then the floors and the Thebans' pots.
  "Don't touch the oil." Takes the lamp away (lamp oil is lamp oil); later throws a goat-hair blanket in
  from the dark ("Blanket"). **She never asks Mara's name.** No thanks, no sitting, no proverb, no
  speech about herself. Her son's room is NOT shown (held for Ch.7).
- **Ch.6 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; backfill, the afternoon and night before the climb):** buys a string of
  split oak from Valens in the lane. Her own life, not Mara's: stands with her back to the pomegranate trunk, arms
  crooked out, to keep the gray mule out of her court ("She had that tree to the height of her head last winter");
  smells the cut end of a split ("Wet." / "It'll spit. The last lot spat a coal on a Theban's cloak, and I paid for
  the cloak." / "Then it'll spit oak."); haggles ("Four now, and the fifth when I've seen it burn without
  spitting"); pays from a knot in her kerchief corner, then goes at him in Phocian too fast for Mara to follow
  (he answers a word at a time) until he gives her one coin back, and ties the knot up again more slowly than she
  untied it. (Synced to the final page 2026-10-19; every other quote in this block is on the page.) Asks Valens whether the
  sea is shut and whether anything came in from the east before the last ship left Kirrha, then "Letters." /
  "Nobody asked what you carry." (her son; unexplained on the page). Of Mara: "Don't wait for an answer. She
  doesn't talk." Orders: "You. Under the stair. Bark up." To a Theban on the gallery: "When the hearth comes."
  That night, from the dark at the top of the outside stair as Mara leaves with the duffel and her ash shovel:
  "The shovel comes back." Mara thanks her for the blanket in English; Kallia: "Shut the gate behind you." Still
  never touches Mara, never asks her name, never speaks of herself.
- **Ch.7 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; winter AD 70-71. SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish,
  disruptor, continuity repair, polish cycle 1 and the three finalize fixes; every quote below is on the page, checked
  by script):** takes the shovel back ("Let the god put an edge
  back on it.") and says nothing more about the mountain that morning. Dresses Mara in the runaway girl's tunic, cord
  and himation ("She took herself off when the grapes came in, in what she stood up in." / "Not to carry water in. The
  hem hides the boots if you walk right. You don't walk right.", one unbroken speech since cycle 1, with no exit inside
  it). Gives her lessons in the court in the slack hour, ignoring the Thebans' advice; after the cheese-man she "stood
  square before me with a pot in her hands" for the eye lesson.
  Lessons as tasks: walking ("Shorter. From the knee. You're not going up a mountain." / "So the town says." / "You see
  their feet." / "You've no ears. I answer."), SITS once to demonstrate (the only time on the page; up before the stool
  stops rocking: "You sit like you paid for the bench."), eyes, given as ONE speech with no beat inside it ("He was
  talking to me. Look at a man out there and you've asked him something. And somebody answers." / "In here you look at
  the pots."). At the bath she washes standing and lies flatly for Mara ("Singe your own," to the baker's wife; "They
  keep them like that where she's from." / "Over the sea." / "Pox," … "When she was small." / "It took one on her." /
  "Your bread's burning, or your husband's mother is eating it."). Corrects Mara's Greek by saying the word back the
  town's way, with no explanation ("Because it's what they're called."); there is no hand-off in it. Her only questions
  about Mara's world are labor: "Where you come from," … "who carries the water?" / "And the wool?" / "What do the women
  do all day?" / "Who pays for a pot that's broken?" / "There's no such country." / "Then why aren't you in it?" (her
  one "Then" retort in Ch.7) / "I want that basket done before the lamp goes." Of the spring's talk: "Let them. Simple
  girls don't get asked anything. Tighter than that; you're making a bird's nest." Asked who slept in the shut room
  upstairs: "It's swept," then "Tighter, I said" (her deflection is the labor she does on the room). The swept door:
  every morning before her own threshold, the broom left lying across it, the rest of the house done "with the other
  one"; she puts it back without a word when Mara borrows it. With Valens: withholds the fifth coin ("It spat." /
  "Twice. Once on my good hearthstone and once in the lentils." / "keep the gray's teeth off my tree"); "She's simple."
  The broken jar: she looks past the pieces at Mara's wrist; "Who?" / "Did he touch you?" (Mara: "Only my wrist.") /
  "That jar came up from Kirrha on my own head, eleven summers ago, and I've not had to buy one since. Go back down and
  fetch the water in the old one. It leaks." Then she sweeps the pieces into the ash pail with her toe (the section no
  longer closes on her water order). That night: the Syracuse plan ("You're my husband's brother's girl." / "From
  Syracuse." / "West, by water. Past Corinth, past everywhere." / "Your people died, and you came on one of the last
  boats before the sea shut, after the vintage."). Mara asks "What was he called? Your husband's brother." and Kallia
  talks straight past it ("They talk strange over there; nobody here has heard enough of it to say how strange. If he
  asks you anything, you've a cold."); the question is never answered. Then "The whole spring knows you came on a
  boat. Bank the hearth before you lie down." At the council: "Free." / "My husband's brother's girl. From Syracuse."
  / "She came on one of the last boats, after the vintage." / "Half Syracuse is Roman," / "I do." / "Who stands for my
  house, if I don't? You've had no other name on that line for years. Write me." She has NEVER asked Mara's name and
  has none to give the clerk; she hears it there, standing beside Mara. At home: "What did he put?" / "Say your name
  for me." / "Maira. The water." (said the Delphian way, nearer *Mera*: the first time she uses a name for Mara). Last
  scene: "At midwinter the women climb Parnassus." / "They go every other winter, and this is the winter. You'll come.
  You've the boots for it." / "Bind your feet in rags over the boots, and bring the girl's himation." / to "And what is
  it for?": "Dance. Drink. Freeze. Not as high as your cave." (one line; the beat that split it was cut). Still never
  touches Mara; still never speaks of herself (the jar and the house only).

- **Ch.8 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; the night on Parnassus. SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19, after
  dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity repair and polish cycle 1; every quote below is on the page, checked by
  script):** opens mid-instruction, down on her heels at the hearthstone binding her own shoe in blanket strips, "her
  hands did it while her eyes were on the pot": "And once we're above the last house you say nothing about men, not
  husbands and not sons, whatever they've done to you this winter," … "Up there you talk about your feet, or the cold, or
  you hold your tongue. Wind it harder than that, or it'll be round your ankle before you're halfway up the stair." ("not
  sons" is hers to say and she does not explain it; the order-or-you'll-fall-before-a-place threat is this one, hers,
  once.) To "Why nothing about men?": "Because it's the mountain. Tear another; there's more blanket than there is
  evening." To "And if they ask me things?": "They'll ask you everything, every step up and every step down." / "You tell
  them what I told the clerk, and not a word over." (Ch.7: Mara gave the clerk only her name; Kallia supplied the rest.)
  To the Thebans' heckle: "The month's due on the day, wine or none," … "If the house burns down, you'll pay for the
  house." (her money axis; the lentil reply is gone). To "Where on the plain do they dance?": "Where they danced the last
  time." and then she moves the pin, "two fingers on the bronze and none on me" (no line with it: the draft's hitch-up order was cut;
  still never touches Mara). In the lane: "Nikaso," … "you've a sieve of mine to mend before you borrow anything else in
  this lane." / "When she likes." / "Do it at the top, where it's flat," … "I'll not lay you out on a stair." Her one
  "Then" retort: "Then you should have borrowed her boots." **Carrying is hers:** she climbs with the wineskin slung
  across her back on a cord ("Kallia carries it and I hold it", Nikaso), and her labor axis on the rite is "Somebody has
  to carry him, as somebody hauls the water." (the eval flags it as a near-proverb, #16; her card strikes proverbs, so no
  second such line). **Dances** with her kerchief off and her gray hair down to her shoulder blades ("I had seen it only
  once before, plastered flat at the bath"), "stiff-elbowed, with her chin up, and she danced as she scoured a pot, all of
  her at once, with no expression on her face" (the rarity gloss is spent on this: her hair; the second gloss, on the hold of her himation, was cut,
  so rarity glosses are at 1). After the cave she looks at "the knees of the himation, which were black with wet" (the
  knees only; the pitch is gone) and goes "to help Philista get the women into a line" (her one walk-off, no "Then" in
  it). On the way: "Your knees are wet through," / "In there." / "Did you find what you went for?" (her one question
  about the cave; Mara: "No. It was shut."). **The Oracle (opens toward Ch.9):** "there's a god in the town who answers
  questions. He doesn't answer them in winter. Once the road's clear of snow he sits down in his house and answers
  whoever's paid." / "Women can't go in to him. A man goes in for you, with your question in his mouth, and brings you out
  the answer." / "Whoever you can get." / "It costs." / "More than you've got. Ask a priest. He'll tell you more than it
  is." **Her varied reply (per the Ch.7 note):** to "Have you asked him anything? The god?" she answers with a question
  back, "Who would I send in?", and no chore follows: "She walked on, and the wind took her flame flat and let it go
  again." To Mara's question about the god in the basket: "He rides down on her head in the morning, and they put him
  back in his house till the next time." **In the pen she crouches, never sits** ("Kallia crouched beside me with her shoes
  off": her ONE posture beat in the chapter since the 2026-10-19 kneel/crouch trim; later she simply "let it go by her
  like weather"), her rags turned to the fire "like flatbreads on a
  griddle"; says nothing through the farce, then fishes a sandal out of the fire with a burning fennel stalk, flicks it
  clear and feeds the stalk to the fire ("And who's going down that stair barefoot in the morning? Who's to carry her?");
  backs Mara ("Give it her, and hush," … "She'll do it if you hush."); "Kallia was no help" over the missing husband (she
  "did not add one now"); Sostrata's eye goes past her without a verse, "which suited Kallia"; at Mara's joke she "bent
  her face over her rags and did not raise it". **Lies down** at the outer edge of the heap, on her side, wind at her back,
  dried rags under her cheek: "Wake me when it's light," … "Those Thebans will have the pot scraped dry by now, and burnt
  the scrapings." Never speaks of herself.

- **Ch.9 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; one short scene at home. SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19, after
  dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity repair and polish cycle 1; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):**
  "Kallia was in the court at home, scouring the big pot with sand." No look at Mara's hand first (continuity W-06 cut it as the
  Ch.7 broken-jar template). Her only question is "Into your hand?" (cycle 1 cut "Who paid?" / "Euthydikos." as the Ch.7
  hearthstone skeleton). To "Onto it. From above." **her varied reply is an act, not a chore and not a question back:** "To
  that she gave no word at all. She filled the basin at the jar and stood it on the hearthstone beside the coins. A lump of the
  gray washing clay went down next to it." Her one chore beat: "The sand hissed round the belly of the pot." To the god's price
  (one line from Mara, no list): "Who takes the goat up?" / "Then you'll want a man a goat will walk for. They don't, for most."
  (her one "Then" retort; her labor axis). Close: "Kallia's sand had gone quiet while I tied it." (she stops work to watch the
  rag knotted; no turn-away, no walk-off). Never sits, never touches Mara, never speaks of herself. No broom, no shut room, no
  letter (Ch.10's). **Ch.10 is hers:** no gaze-arrival "then" for her (eval Cross-Book #4), no hearthstone exchange in the Ch.7/9
  shape, no body rebel through Mara's hands.

- **Ch.10 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19; one afternoon at home. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish,
  hook-craft, the disruptor, continuity repair, polish cycle 1 and the PATH fix; every quote below is on the page, checked by
  script):** takes the letter at the gate ("Kallia had it out of his fingers and held it shut in her hand.") and asks only
  "Where from?"; to the Elean's "He said it might be bad": "Her face gave him nothing for that." To the Elean, money and labor:
  "Water," (to Mara: the promised cup, paid cheaply) / "That was a cup." / "Then drink in Elis." (her one "Then" retort) /
  "Full." / "The house is full." / "There's the houses by the stadium." **Carries the letter unread in her belt from noon to
  mid-afternoon** ("She pushed the letter down behind her belt, against her stomach, and returned to the pots."; "The corner of
  it showed over her belt when she reached up for the oil."; "Once, going past the jar, she touched the place through her
  clothes."); gives the man from Patrae his bread "twice over" (a slip, unremarked). **Stands to spin** "beside the lean-to that
  held the hearth and all the kitchen the house had", and first, as plain seasonal work on "the first afternoon of the year with
  heat enough in the sun to work without a cloak", lets down the lean-to's summer blind (moved here by polish cycle 1 so that it
  answers nothing): a reed blind "rolled up under the eave"; she "pulled the slip-knot on its cord one-handed. The blind dropped
  open to the ground between the jar and the sun, letting down a winter's soot and a dead wasp. She came back to her place."
  "Presently she let it hang." (the spindle; a hand freed for the letter). Holds the letter toward Mara "across the wool at the
  length of her arm": "You read the clerk's wax," (her only acknowledgment that Mara reads). Her replies during the reading:
  "There's gods enough here," / "Which arm?" / "He was never any use with the left," / "Two and a half," … "At the vintage." /
  "Is she Greek?" / "Can she weave?" / "Anybody keeps a house." … "Go on." / "Here it's for the father's father." (left
  unanswered) / **the yawn** (involuntary, face and eyes, twice; Mara takes it for tiredness) / "A roof you sit on," … "Where does
  the rain go?" / "They'll have enough." / "The man," … "Bought?" / "The middle of winter's gone by." / "It's come, then,
  whichever it is." / answering the greetings to the letter itself ("She was answering the letter"): "She's living," …
  "Thrasyllos died when the olives were in, the year after. *Let me take*, he says. He took them, at ten years old, and told
  nobody, and Thrasyllos walked to Kirrha after him in his bare feet. Glauke's girls are married. The big one's in Amphissa with a
  potter, treading his clay for him." (her one memory of him, given as neighbor news). At the letter's end, stillness: "Kallia
  stood in the sun with the wool in her hand." To Mara's "He says the arm has knit," no word: "She drew a fresh pull of wool from
  the hank, and let it run back." / "Which is his name?" ("She looked at it from where she stood, and came no nearer.") / "Keep
  it out of the smoke," / to Nikaso, flat: "He's living." / "He sends you greeting. You and your husband." / "He's married, in
  Syria, and there's a child." / "That Elean was promised a cup of wine for carrying it, and got water." (no errand) / to "How
  old was he, when he went?": a task, "Put a plate on those, or the mice will be in." (her one chore-reply in the chapter) / to
  Mara's offer to write back: "On what?" / then a question nobody asked (the disruptor's interruption, left unexplained): "The
  horse-seller," … "His name again." (Mara: "Iamblichos.") / "And whoever carries it to Syria?" / "Tell him," (unfinished; she
  "wound the yarn up, all of it") / at the foot of the stair: "Tell him the baker's old mother is living." / an obol from her belt
  "on the bottom stair, where I would have to pass it." … "For the sheet." **The broom (anchor):** "Kallia lifted the broom off
  its boards and swept them. At dawn they had been swept already; I had been carrying out the ashes when she did them." "She
  took it to the lip of the gallery in short strokes, and nothing came over, and back to the door. Once more she went over them,
  along the grain", then "laid the broom over the threshold again, the head against one post and the handle against the other,
  and came down." Her good himation: "She covered her head with it, kerchief and all, and closed it under her chin." **Says his
  name once**: "That Elean will have been all round the stadium by now," … "and the houses there are full to the tiles. He can
  have Ariston's room, at the three obols. If he comes to the gate before I find him, he waits in the court." Exit by the gate
  only ("The gate came to behind her and bounced once."; no gait or footwear), and "Up at the last door the broom still lay over
  the threshold, from post to post." There is no exchange about opening the room. **Never sits, never touches Mara, no
  gaze-arrival "then", no hearthstone shape, no bending, no kerchief-knot payment, never speaks of herself.** Leaves the letter
  with Mara (it goes in the niche "where Kallia kept the good knife and a twist of cumin"). Page counts: chore-replies 1;
  errands 0; name-asks 2; work-suspended tell 2 expressive uses (the wool in her hand; the pull run back) plus the functional
  "Presently she let it hang."; "living" ×3 in her mouth (×5 in the chapter) and "cup" ×6, both CLOSED after Ch.10.

- **Ch.11 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; offstage but for one evening at the hearth. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20: her
  scene is byte-identical through every pass; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** she sends Mara to Praxias on
  the Kirrha road "before the lamps were out" (off the page). After the lame-mule night: "She stood over the pot with the ladle, and
  gave me only the side of her face as I came past her." / "That yard's on your himation," … "Whose water washes it?" (her labor
  axis, as a question back; not a chore-reply; Mara: "Mine. I will carry it.") / "Is he paying you?" (Mara: "No.") / "The ladle made
  its circuit of the pot." / "There's women in this lane twenty years married who'd not do that much for a man for nothing." (the
  chapter's one spoken romance signal, given to her, not the narration). Never sits, never touches Mara, never speaks of herself;
  chore-replies 0; "Then" retorts 0; walk-offs 0; no wool, broom or spindle; no name-ask. Praxias on her: "Kallia never asks for
  herself." She has NOT been shown the notice or connected it to Valens (keep her from it). Her obol for the papyrus is still unspent.

- **Ch.13 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20, polish cycle 1 + FZ1–FZ3; every quote below is on the page,
  checked by script):** **Given the whole story** at the lions before light (she had only the donkey man's robbery, and "kept it to
  herself"); sends a yawning Thessalian "to the far trough with four words"; **spits "quickly, into the fold of her dress at the breast"**
  at the man's death (once; unexplained; with Ch.4's goat-boy that is 2 book-wide, CLOSED); at the notice, "Five hundred," very low; her one
  question is about the worker in the story: "The girl that dressed the wife's hair," … "What became of her?" / "Sold on, or sent out to his
  farm." … "A girl who's said that sort of thing in an atrium doesn't stay in the house after to do the hair." **Lets Valens into her
  court:** "Bring him to the court after; the Thessalians will be up at the sanctuary till dark, and there's a pot of lentils wants eating."
  / "The floors will keep," … "Be home ahead of the Thessalians, or you'll be on the stair half the night with their dinners." Puts him "on
  the stool by the woodstack with the wall at his back" and sets the bowl in his hands (no word about the court). From the lean-to:
  "Euthydikos," / "Somebody was paid to paint that door." … "Somebody's sons paid him, and argued the price." (labor axis on the god's
  answer) / "The boats go west at the new moon." (the subtext of a leaving, said as a fact). **Keeps Valens the night**, standing over him
  with the pot on her hip: "You'll not go down that road tonight," … "The boy can sleep with the string. He'd sooner. You'll sleep here." …
  "There's floor," / of Theano: "The god's woman," … "Up the street, almost to the end of it. She'll have you carry her water up while
  you're there. She does that." / "She'll not eat her," (to Valens). Never sits, never touches Mara, never speaks of herself; chore-replies
  0; "Then" retorts 0; name-asks 0; no broom, wool, spindle or stopped work.

- **Ch.14 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20. SYNCED 2026-10-20: FINAL page, polish cycle 1 + FZ1–FZ7; every quote below is on the page, checked
  by script):** "Kallia asked me nothing." She "put an end of bread in my hand and the scrapings of the pot in a bowl"; has laid Valens's

  sick Thessalian boy: "Kallia was over them with a cup. She gave me the lamp to hold and went for salt." (labor, no order echoed); "The boy
  drank the salt water and was sick with it again. Kallia filled the cup again."; "He ate the fat off the god's share. His own and his
  brothers'," ; "Kallia emptied the cup into the drain and took back the lamp." / "What did Theano want with you?" (her one question; it

  down for her," … "It isn't their knees." (who carries: her axis). **Her blanket over Valens's feet:** "She went into her room and was back

  threshold, folded in four."; she has done Mara's work ("The ashes had been carried out and the hearth brushed, which was my work, and the
  two water jars stood full beside the wood, sweating."); "Kallia stood at the gate, ready to lift the bar." / "He's gone down for the
  mules," … "You're to wait for him at the bottom of the climb. There's bread in the cloth." / "She took the bar down and let me into the
  lane." (she heard the cave errand from the lean-to in Ch.13; never said). Never sits; never touches Mara; no broom, no wool, no stopped
  work; chore-replies 0. **Kallia hands Mara a thing: 2 in Ch.14** (the bread, the lamp): WATCH, ≤1 in Ch.15–16. **Spent in Ch.14:** the
  salt-water nursing at the drain; the blanket dropped over a sleeper's feet; doing Mara's chores unasked.
- **Ch.15 (backfill only, Writer, 2026-10-20. SYNCED 2026-10-20: FINAL page, polish cycle 1 + the CH15-1 spring line + Z1–Z6; quotes

  listening": "Gone," … "Gone to Kirrha at the turn of the month and onto a boat for home, the god go with her. She ate more than she
  carried, that one, and broke two of my jars." **This is a cover told to the lane:** the jars are not a fact (ENTITY CH15-9), and she

  thing smaller than I would have folded it", and has one line, on the labor axis: "On the mountain you'll milk for your bread, or carry
  for it,". **Kallia hands Mara an object: 0 in Ch.15** (she packs the bag; nothing is put in Mara's hand), so the WATCH (≤1 in Ch.15–16)
  still has its one use for Ch.16. Mara's picture of her at the pot telling the bean-sheller "the Syracusan had sailed" is imagined, not
  a scene. **The Ch.16 farewell must be covert** (Delphi believes Mara sailed; continuity W-12).
- **Ch.16 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; the night return and first light, autumn AD 71. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21:

  - **Her own life first:** "Kallia's gate stood ajar." She is "in the gap holding the lamp low, a sandal in her other fist, studying

    "There's its brother in the woodstack somewhere," (not about Mara). "Kallia held the gate for me, and barred it behind." / "She put
    her foot in the sandal and worked the heel down." The gallery cough is heard **once**, in D1 ("Overhead somebody coughed in his

  - **Lines (D1 only; D2 has NO speech, as the outline orders):** "Did he bring them all down?" / "The boy's going?" / "When's the
    boat?" / "He fed you, up there?" / "Burnt underneath, the bread." / "His mother never showed him a griddle," … "or he never

  - **The himation given outright (NON-NEGOTIABLE, no speech):** Mara folds it "lengthwise and then across, the darned corner inside"
    and gives it "to her past the lamp"; "Kallia looked at it. She took it from me by its folds, as she would have taken a pile of a
    lodger's bedding, and carried it into her own room, and the lamp with it"; "Her shutter stood a crack open on the court. The light
    stayed in it." By first light it lies "folded on my bag inside the door", **worked overnight**: "Kallia had closed it into a round
    eye, working a gray thread about it", with a second thread run "through the weave all round"; the hem "turned where the scree and
    the thorn had frayed it, from one corner to the other, and stitched down"; "Toward the second corner they grew a fraction longer,

  - **The sweep and the broom (NON-NEGOTIABLE, third wording; the broom's position is not described):** "She came out carrying the

  - **The named frame (NON-NEGOTIABLE):** "The court grew light, and the flame on the sill went from being the brightest thing there to
    being only one of the things in it." Her lamp on her window sill, burning since the night, "a round belly of brown clay, a nozzle
