  the page; "One of the men at the mast laughed."), then "making it fast to a pin"; "a rowing boat" off the bow with "a slack line
  running from its stern to the ship, and the rowers were eating"; they get to their feet and cheer the kicked sailor ("Give him
  another!" one of them shouted.); the boat does not rock. "On the mole somebody was lifting the stern rope off its post." **The quay:**
  across the basin "a man sat in a beached boat mending a net and whistling, as if the afternoon were his to spend" (R1; the chapter's

  "she read the brooch before she read the face"; she "heard her out to the end and answered her in Greek, quick Greek, the Greek of
  the quays, of which I caught perhaps half: that the house was up at the highest part of the town, that there was fish, and the girl
  thinner than she had been told. The question she put next was about us." (the reported-speech triad broken at its third clause,

- **The Puteoli harbor clerk (the outline's role; UNNAMED, functional):** under an awning "Where the mole met the land", a table "with
  tablets stacked at one end", and "a slave behind him holding a case of styluses" (who "moved the case of styluses into the crook of
  the other arm"); "long in the neck, with his hair combed forward, and there was a smudge of black on the tip of his nose". **His
  object-to-the-eye beat: one bearer, 4 instances in one scene** (the repetition is the evidence Mara argues from): the master's list,
  "so near that the wax all but touched the end of his nose"; the leaf, "The clerk lifted it to his nose and read it there, slowly,
  one board and then the other. He left it lying open,"; his own tablet, "The clerk brought his tablet up to his nose and read over
  what he had written."; a sailor's tablet, "the clerk had a sailor's tablet up against his face". **CLOSED for Ch.20: no walk-on
  there holds anything to the eye.** CUT: "two-handed". Lines: "Beasts for sale?" / "And that?" (the fig, "on the stones at Felix's
  feet, jar and all", continuity W-07) / "Your name." / "Freedman of whom?" (not a third whose-question; CUT: "Whose freedman?") /
  "Corinth," … "Nero's year." … "That was a lucky year for some. And the woman?" / "And the lad?" / "Plenty," (waves them on). He
  comes out from behind his table "to peer up into his face from nearer than I had ever seen one man look at another in a street":
  short sight, not recognition; Valens reads it as being marked. **Not hostile; the papers work.** Two of the chapter's three
  "And…" rejoinders are his (the wine-woman has the third). If he recurs, he gets a name and a fresh physical mark; the nose smudge
  is spent.
- **Pacuvius at Puteoli:** stands at the clerk's shoulder telling him his cargo "quicker than he could read"; vouches: "His," …
  "Married at Lechaion, on the stones, in front of my crew, and I'll swear to it here or anywhere you like."; the fleet out of
  Misenum ("they'll sit him on a bench under the deck where he never sees the sky, and he can row the admiral round this bay till his
  beard comes in gray. Go and ask."); the letter board ("every master on this run leaves his letters on that board, and not one of us
  carries a letter a step past it, me least of all. That's the last I'll say to you."); "He was back up his own plank before anyone
  had thanked him." Evening libation on the open sea ("poured some of it on the water, and prayed in a voice he never used on us").
  On the quay he goes down "behind her for once instead of at the rail". He never says his daughter's name. The sandal is not
  mentioned. **Spent: Pacuvius, done; he does not recur unless the letter thread needs a master, and then in new words.**
- **The wine-woman at the mole's foot (Puteoli):** keeps the ships' letter board ("tied up in thread and tagged with the names of
  whoever had still to fetch them"). Valens asks aloud ("Anything that comes by sea from Corinth for Valens the muleteer," … "keep it
  on your board for me."): "Who'll it be from?" / "And where do I send when it comes? I don't send, mind. Somebody comes for it, or it
  stays on the board till it's eaten." Valens: "A lady's brother." / "Pompeii," … "I'll send to you." **OPEN THREAD:** the brother's

- **Puteoli texture (once each):** "The quay was crowded from the water's edge to the warehouse doors."; the porters on the
  Alexandrians' planks ("grain dust from the sacks settled in their hair"); "A man was selling something fried in oil out of a pan on
  a brazier, and a priest in white linen with his head shaved was arguing with him about it." (no language detail: R13 + F2); "A dog
  slept at the foot of a plank, and the porters stepped round it."; the grain ships from Alexandria, "broader than anything at
  Lechaion", that "carried a little three-cornered sail high on the mast above the yard" (CUT: "which no other ship in the harbor
  flew"); the mole's statues of "men in togas streaked white by gulls" and its piers, where "the sea had eaten the brick off the
  lowest courses and bared the gray core, packed full of pebbles".
- **The torch boys at the tunnel (the road through the hill to Neapolis):** "Light you through, mistress? A copper, and you'll see your
  own feet." / to Felix: "You can carry your mule," the torch boy said. Two hired.
- **The old man in Neapolis:** "Footraces," … "and the lyre, and poets. Every four years, for the emperor." (the Neapolitan games;
  "Felix left him with his face screwed up tight."). **The balcony woman** (Greek "with its vowels drawn out longer"): "Come in off

- **The innkeeper near Neapolis (east of the city, by a little river in reeds):** "a ring of keys at her belt": "Mats upstairs, two
  coppers a head. The beasts are four the lot, and the boy sleeps with them or pays for a mat like anybody. Money first. The last lot
  left in the night and owed me." A fig grows in her yard (Valens walks round it once; no fig opinion).
- **The two carters from Nola (the loft):** cheese for Puteoli, "smelling of it"; one, "slow with sleep and muffled by his cloak":
  "Some of us," … "have cheese to sell on the Puteoli quay by sunup, and a wife there who can tell from the cheese what time I sold
  it." Their news, through Felix: the school for fighters, the amphitheater, the Nucerian riot ("They fought it out on the benches.

  the reader's history, unglossed). Felix buys a hard cheese from them.
- **Road walk-ons:** "a slave stood in a little boat in the middle of the pond scattering something"; the woman on a high threshing
  floor who "took up her flail again"; the farm-gate wine (pitch-lined jar); the salt-pan rakers "in loincloths".
- **Pompeii walk-ons, day of arrival:** the mason on a ladder pointing a brick patch, "singing to himself" (the song "dropped down the

  it); "soldered down its length". **Mention only:** "Marcus Satrius Proculus", candidate for aedile on a white panel ("the muleteers
  asked you to make" him aedile, "and that he was a man worthy of it"); Grata's cousin the bad plasterer; Grata's husband's brother
  ("never been off" a ship); the cobbler (dead, owed Grata the winter) and his nephew from Nola; the fuller and his wife; the baker
  over the way; the man in Nuceria who owns the propped house; the woman at the fountain with the blue jar; the contractor at the

- **The cobbler's shop (as found):** "The floor was beaten earth," with "a wooden last, too broken to be worth taking, with a cobweb

- **Grata's husband (the outline's Ch.24/31 role; first glimpse, unnamed):** "In the opening of her own shop next door a man in a

## MINOR NAMED CHARACTERS (all four axes filled; the Writer extends each card on first appearance)

#### PHILOKRATES — Greek physician in Pompeii (Ch.32)
- **Attention / worldview:** signs in the body (skin, breath, urine, sweat) and what precedent says;
  patient Hippocratic care. He has seen many children die and some live.
- **Vocabulary band:** educated Greek medical vocabulary (*krisis*, the humors), courteous; he moves into
  Greek when he argues with Mara.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** **explains everything, kindly, in the first-person plural of the sickroom**
  ("we cool him," "we watch"); speaks of the patient in the third person. **No aphorisms or Hippocratic
  quotations used as lines** (retired reflex).
- **Rhythm:** measured, unhurried, repeating instructions in fresh words when the parents are frightened.
- **Evasion — never says:** any promise about the outcome.
- **Physicality / silence:** smells the child's breath; warm, dry hands; washes his hands in wine; sits on
  the floor by the pallet.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by syntax (patient explanation, sickroom "we") and evasion (no
  promises)
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the calm man who explains everything, against Valens at the same bedside,
  who explains nothing.
- **Sample line:** "We will not bleed him, since you ask it so loudly; honey in warm water, a little
  vinegar, cloths from the cistern and not the well, and then we listen to how he breathes."

#### SECUNDUS — Grata's son, candidate for aedile, AD 77 (Ch.31; mentioned Ch.20–31)
- **Attention / worldview:** office, respectability, the family name on a wall; he wants not to be the son
  of a woman who sells lentils.
- **Vocabulary band:** borrowed civic grandeur (the duumvirs, the *ordo*, "the good men of the street");
  rehearsed.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** campaign periods, a speech he has practiced, collapsing into his mother's
  street Latin when crossed.
- **Rhythm:** a rehearsed line, then a scramble.
- **Evasion — never says:** where the campaign money comes from; his father's dice.
- **Physicality / silence:** clean toga worn too carefully; glances at his mother before answering.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by vocabulary band (borrowed civic grandeur) and evasion (the money)
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the young man performing a speech, against his mother, who has never
  performed anything in her life.
- **Sample line:** "Every vote on this street is a vote for a clean fountain, and I mean to tell the
  duumvirs so to their faces. Mother, is the paint dry?"

#### ARISTON — Kallia's son, auxiliary veteran in Emesa, Syria (Ch.10, by letter only; Mara's reply to him written Ch.16)
- **Attention / worldview:** his new life (a wife, a child coming, discharge land); the neighbors he
  remembers by name.
- **Vocabulary band:** soldier's Koine with the formulas of a Greek letter ("Ariston to his mother Kallia,
  very many greetings. Before all things I pray you are well…").
- **Syntax fingerprint:** epistolary formula, a dutiful run of greetings, then plain news.
- **Rhythm:** formula, news, formula.
- **Evasion — never says:** "home," "return," or anything about his room; he greets the neighbors and not
  the room.
- **Physicality / silence:** none on the page; the letter itself is his body (folded small, stained from
  the road).
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by vocabulary band (letter formulas) and evasion (no "home")
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only voice that reaches the book entirely in letter formula, against
  Fenn's English letters, which have no formula at all.
- **Sample line:** "Greet Philon the potter for me, and his wife, and old Damo by the spring, and tell them
  the sun here is a thing they would not believe."
- **Status: MENTION-ONLY (by letter).** He never appears; his voice reaches the page only as the letter Mara reads aloud.
  Verbal tic: none — distinguished by vocabulary band (Greek letter formula) and evasion (no "home", no room, no return).
- **Ch.10 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish, hook-craft, the
  disruptor, continuity repair, polish cycle 1 and the PATH fix; every quote is on the page, checked by script):** the letter
  "had been folded down very small"; "One end of it had stood in wet somewhere and dried with a brown tidemark across it. The
  thread round its middle was knotted under a thumb of gray clay." (polish cycle 1 cut the material sentence, the hand-ruler and
  the fold count, which rebuilt Ch.9's curse tablet). Sent from Emesa "on the twentieth of Hyperberetaios" (autumn AD 70), it "came
  over from Corinth on a coaster at the back end of the sailing, and then the sailing stopped", hung all winter "on a nail behind
  his jars" at a Kirrha wine-seller's, and came up with the Elean pilgrim. Opened: "Unfolded, the papyrus was mostly creases and
  needed both my hands to keep it flat. The hand inside was small and level from top to foot." (a scribe's or his own: the page
  does not say). **Formula:** "Ariston to Kallia, his mother, greeting, and many of them. Before everything I pray for your health,
  and every day I make an offering for you to the gods of this place, though you would not know their names." **News:** "Know that
  I was with the cohort before Jerusalem from springtime until the city was taken. I have come out of it alive and whole, all but
  the left arm, which a stone off the wall broke above the elbow. It has knit, but it will not straighten. On account of it the
  prefect has given me my discharge before my time, and paid me out what I had saved at the standards." (savings at the
  standards, not a bounty: continuity W-04); "Forgive me the two years." (Kallia: "Two and a half," … "At the vintage."); "Nobody
  was going west, and after that there was the war. The year before it I took a wife here: Shalmath, the daughter of Iamblichos,
  who sells horses to the cohort. She is a good wife to me, and keeps my house well. Her father has no son, and he calls me his.";
  "She is with child. Her mother says it will come before the middle of the winter. If it is a boy, he will be named for her
  father, as they do here."; "In the evenings the whole house goes up on the roof. From there you can see the river, and the
  mountains past it, which keep their snow until the summer."; "With the money from the cohort and what came with her, I have
  bought land on the river half a day below Emesa. It is wheat land, with olives along the bank already, and water runs past the
  door in a channel all summer. I have a yoke of oxen, and a man to drive them, since I cannot." **Greetings by name:** "Greet for
  me Nikaso and her husband. Tell Philista I have not forgotten whose drum it was I put my foot through. Greet the baker and
  Sostrata, and his old mother, if she is living, and Thrasyllos who let me take his mules to the harbor, and Glauke's girls at
  the spring, both of them." **Close:** "I pray for your health, mother, and that the gods keep you. Farewell. Written at Emesa, on
  the twentieth of Hyperberetaios." Address on the back: "to Kallia, at Delphi, at the lodging above the Castalian spring, from her
  son." **Nothing for the house, the room or a return** ("For the house there was no line, nor for anything under its roof.").
  No kin in Syracuse is mentioned (the register's "Aimilios of Syracuse" stands untouched). The letter ends in the lean-to niche,
  "in behind the cumin". Canon now fixed: wife **Shalmath** (not Domna), daughter of Iamblichos (horse dealer to the cohort); a
  child born by midwinter AD 70-71, sex unknown to Delphi; discharged early for the left arm, paid out his savings at the
  standards; land below Emesa on the river (the Orontes, unnamed on the page). The architect's sample wordings above ("very many
  greetings", "Before all things") are calibration only and are not on the page.
- **Ch.16 status (SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21; quotes checked by script): the reply is WRITTEN, not yet sent.** At the gate
  Mara tells Kallia "I will buy a sheet there and write it, and find somebody going to Syria." / Kallia: "Pay him when he's aboard," …
  "Not before." It is written in Corinth "in Plautia's room four mornings after that first one, on a sheet of papyrus from a stall at
  the top of the Lechaion road", copied out twice ("the first one blotted"). Its content is told, never quoted: "It is the news of her
  lane." / Plautia: "Nothing of herself." / "Not a word." Mara to Plautia: "A woman in Delphi, to her son. He lives in Syria now." /
  "He has written once," … "in more than three years." He is not named on the Ch.16 page. **The obol:** "I have your obol still," …
  "The sheet was to come from Euthydikos's people, and then it was the mountain." (the page means the priest's fee would buy the sheet;
  Ch.17 and Ch.18 never re-explain the obol). Who carries the reply, and whether it reaches Emesa: OPEN.

#### MARCUS — Plautia's son, b. autumn AD 64; aged 7 in Plautia's mouth (Ch.16, mention only); aged 9 when glimpsed (Ch.22, spring 74)
- **Attention / worldview:** what he has been told to do; his mother's face, which he is relearning.
- **Vocabulary band:** a well-schooled aristocratic child's Latin, formal beyond his years.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** a sentence he has been taught, then one of his own that comes out too fast.
- **Rhythm:** stiff, then sudden.
- **Evasion — never says:** anything about his father.
- **Physicality / silence:** holds his mother's veil-edge; stands very straight.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by syntax (taught formula, then a child's rush)
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the child performing manners, against Lucius, who is grave without having
  been taught to be.
- **Sample line:** "Mother says I am to thank you. Thank you. Is that man really a gladiator?"
- **Ch.16 (mention only, in Plautia's mouth; SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21; quotes checked by script):** "My son was seven at
  the vintage." / "He is in his father's house, where the law says he must be." / named "Marcus. For his father's father, who held the
  consulship two months in a bad year."; "his father's ears, which stand out, and my mother's temper, which does not"; the Muses "all
  nine, in order", not the Graces ("because he said they were only girls"); seen twice in three years, "once at the theater, from the
  women's benches at the top, and once in the street, being taken to the baths"; the ball "of painted leather, red and green" ("the
  porter at that house took it in at the gate"); the tooth marks on the bracelet ("He had two teeth at the bottom by midsummer," …); at
  the games "in the second row, in a new tunic, beside his father". He has no voice on the page yet: Ch.22's glimpse is his first.
