- **Attention / worldview (as rendered):** the person left behind; she told people he was in Mexico.
- **Vocabulary band:** none of her own on the page; she exists only in Fenn's words.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** **never quoted directly.**
- **Rhythm:** n/a, silence.
- **Evasion — never said (as rendered):** she never sold his boxes while she lived.
- **Physicality / silence:** the hall table where he left the note; the cigar box; the estate sale.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by silence (she is never given a line)
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the one character whose absence is the point, against Fenn, who fills every
  silence but hers. **Her name is spoken aloud by him only once, in Ch.35.**
- **Sample line:** none. She is never quoted.

#### MRS. DUNPHY — substitute teacher in Mara's fourth-grade class (memory only: Ch.7, 24, 41)
- **Attention / worldview (as remembered):** the desk in front of her; the long division she stopped
  teaching.
- **Vocabulary band:** none; the memory has no dialogue.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** she never speaks in her own voice. The one reported line (Ch.7, "said we could go") is the dismissal, and that is all she is allowed.
- **Rhythm:** silence; the class's pencils.
- **Evasion:** why she cried. No one ever learned. **Never connected to the plot.**
- **Physicality / silence:** crying without sound at her desk; a cardigan with a missing button, green in
  Ch.7 and brown in Ch.24; by Ch.41 Mara is unsure it was long division.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by silence (the memory mutates; no direct speech, ever)
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** a silent, mutating memory, against Emilio, a remembered voice. Each surfacing
  enters with a different sentence (never "I thought of Mrs. Dunphy" twice).
- **Sample line:** none. No direct speech; at most one reported line per surfacing.
- **Ch.7 as written (synced to the final page 2026-10-19):** enters "Mrs. Dunphy cried at her desk for a whole
  afternoon when I was in fourth grade, in a green cardigan short of its second button." She stops in the middle of
  a remainder; the class does the problems twice; the paragraph ends on the bell, her wiping her face "with the backs
  of her fingers", and "said we could go". The not-knowing stays implicit: the closing sentence saying nobody in the room
  ever learned why was cut (continuity W-12), and "None of us went for the principal" replaced a "Nobody" opener. Later surfacings
  (Ch.24, Ch.41) must not end on not-knowing either. **After polish cycle 1** the paragraph (now ¶380) is byte-identical, but
  it follows the storeroom scene's walk through the Philadelphia building, so it reads slightly less uninvited than the outline asks (re-eval).
  Ch.24 and Ch.41: let Dunphy arrive straight out of the present scene, never right after another memory or a remembered
  walk.

#### SOSANDROS — clerk to the Delphian council; enters households on the tribute lists (Ch.5 unnamed, "the thin man in the doorway" who corrects the herald; named Ch.7)
- **Attention / worldview:** correct forms. Titles in full, a city's proper name ("Syrakousai"), the right column for a Theban
  ("they're on the Boeotian list"), who pays for the wax. His own comfort comes second, and only just: the cold
  in his feet. He does not care who a person is as long as the line is complete.
- **Vocabulary band:** official Delphian Greek; register-words (bought / born in the house / free, kin, "who
  answers for her"), council business, festival lore ("the Pythian games").
- **Syntax fingerprint:** **the form's questions, in the form's order**, clipped to a word or two ("Name." "Daughter
  of?" "Kin?"); **at most two dry put-downs per encounter (CAP 2)**, delivered without looking up. Ch.7 reaches the
  cap (the wall line to the father, and the wax line with the fee), so a third in one scene is a defect and a later
  encounter must give them a new shape; corrections given as the bare right word ("Syrakousai").
- **Rhythm:** question, write, question; a single longer sentence only when he is pleased with himself (his
  Syracusan at the games).
- **Evasion — never says:** any question he would have to write the answer to twice. He does not ask how a name is
  spelled; he writes MAIRA. He never asks a person anything about herself that the form does not.
- **Physicality / silence:** thin; head down over the wax book; a hot brick wrapped in rag under his feet, fetched
  back from the brazier by a boy; warms each wax leaf over the brazier before he writes. No fidget: the stylus
  moves only to write.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by syntax (the form's questions, in order) and attention (correct forms)
- **Bleed guard:** he must not notice workmanship or materials (Mara reads the wax and the pale letters, not he);
  must not take on Kallia's labor-reckoning (his costs are the council's: wax, fee). His correction move ("the
  bare right word") is spent twice on the page (Ch.5 the titles, Ch.7 the city): **do not give him a third
  correction in the same shape** if he returns; vary.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the official who corrects the name of a city and misspells a woman without asking,
  against the Ch.17 Corinth archive clerk, who is bored, eats figs and corrects nothing.
- **Sample line:** "Bring me the father's name and the city, and spare me the rest; the wax is cold enough."
- **Ch.7 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19. SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish, disruptor,
  continuity repair, polish cycle 1 and the finalize fixes; the council scene is unchanged by cycle 1 apart from ¶348's
  queue line; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** at a table at the end of the council hall beside the square,
  a line of householders before him, the tribute lists (the talk at the spring is that the new emperor means to take
  back Nero's "freedom"; nobody there knows when, and the council wants every house on tablets "before Rome put a
  governor back in Corinth to ask for it"). Lines on the page: "Kallia. The lodging over the spring." / "Boy, the
  brick. Who's in it?" / "Bought, born in the house, or free?" / "Kin?" / "Syrakousai," (correcting her) / "Resident
  since when? On what business?" (the form's next two boxes, clipped: he asks her nothing the form does not) /
  "Name," (to Mara) / to the father with the wall: "Your wall has been coming down through three councils. It will
  stay up for one widow." (put-down 1) / "Sicilian … They all talk like that over there. I heard a Syracusan at the
  Pythian games once, and half the stand had to help him get a cup of water." (his one longer, pleased sentence; no
  performance comparison for her Greek) / "Daughter of?" / "A Roman." / "And who answers for her? A free woman wants
  someone to answer for her." / "A woman can't answer for—" / "Two obols," … "and don't tell me the god pays for wax."
  (put-down 2: the cap) / "Next." Writes MAIRA (alpha, iota) without a question, "as easily as a man spelling a name
  he had known all his life". The entry: *Maira, daughter of Aimilios, of Syrakousai; free; kinswoman of Kallia, of
  her house and in her keeping.* Kallia calls him by name; he calls her by hers. Rendering note (audit N-13, open): in
  English, Kallia's "Syracuse" and his "Syrakousai" are the same word, so the correction has no visible object unless
  a later pass gives Kallia a Phocian form.

#### NIKASO — Kallia's neighbor midway along the lane; the wife of the man with the sieve (Ch.5 off-page, asleep; Ch.8)
- **Attention / worldview:** borrowed things and the ruin coming to them, and who will be blamed (her husband's sister's
  red sandals; Kallia's sieve, returned with a hole). Everything will go wrong, and it will be her fault.
- **Vocabulary band:** quick lane Greek, household and family nouns (sandals, sister, supper, eyes, toes).
- **Syntax fingerprint:** a worry put as a question she answers herself, then a fatal punchline ("If it snows they'll
  stop squeaking, and then I'm a dead woman." / "If it sours in the skin while she's at it, who gets the blame? Me." /
  "and then she has my toes and the sandals both").
- **Rhythm:** fast run-ons that jump subject without a beat (wine to sandals in one speech), a pause for a breath she does
  not have, a short verdict.
- **Evasion — never says:** that the sieve was her fault; any thanks (she puts the mended sandal on, "waited for it to
  fail", and says "Now I'll have one eye").
- **Physicality / silence:** small and quick, a mass of black hair, a nose once broken and mended a little to the left
  (Mara's repairer's eye, not hers), skirts held clear in two fists; holds the wineskin ("Kallia carries it and I hold
  it") and drinks Mara's share.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (borrowed things and blame) and syntax (the worry she answers
  herself). "She'll have my eyes" is CAPPED at the two Ch.8 uses; never again.
- **Bleed guard:** she never notices how a thing is made (Mara's) and never reckons who carries what (Kallia's); her
  sandals are about blame, not leather (dialogue-polish cut a leather proverb and a maxim from her mouth; no proverbs or maxims in her mouth). Her literalism ("It's a cave," … "It's always open.") is spent.
- **Ch.8 as written (SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** comes out
  "Midway along the lane" (continuity CH8-5: Ch.5 puts the sieve door halfway along the lane, Kallia's at its end).
  "Kallia, it's not going to snow, is it? Tell me it's not. I've my husband's sister's sandals on." / "She's no boots. Who
  has boots?" / "She'll have my eyes if they come back spoiled." / "Is she the Syracusan? Does she talk?" / "Who talks
  well, up there? You'll be our third, then, for the wine" / "They've stopped," / "That's the wet getting in, and she'll
  know; she knows if I've so much as breathed on her loom." / "Over new sandals? What's the good of them, wrapped?" / "The
  god," … "And Philista, who leads us up, and she tells everyone at the lions." / **her stair story, told once (cycle 1
  plant; CLOSED):** "Snow caught the women up here once, when my mother was a girl," … "They sent men up in the morning to
  fetch them, and the men's cloaks froze so hard on the climb that they broke in two when anybody tried to unfold them." /
  "Is it true that in Syracuse the women go to the baths with the men?" / "She's never been anywhere. She's never been to
  Kirrha." / at the basket: "Him." … "That's his cradle. You'll see." / "It's bread as well," / "Kallia's dancing. Look at
  her." … "Drink." / "I kept it for you as long as I could," … "Then I couldn't." / "A little was left in the bottom, and
  the skin was getting cold, and either way it was my fault." / "Who sends you up mountains, then?" / in the pen:
  "Cooked, she can't prove anything. Wet, she'll smell them from the lane." / "Who taught you sandals?" / weeps over the
  shrunk red sandal ("She'll have my eyes," … "Both of them. She'll have them out with a spoon.") / "What for? It's ruined.
  Look at it." / "What are you doing to it?" / "How long?" / "Now I'll have one eye," / "Stand it by me," … "It's warmer
  company than his sister, and it's got her shoulders." / "Mine would bring a cold one," … "and ask me where his supper
  was." / "That's a lie," … "He takes them off for the bed." / in the heap: "Maira." … "Where did you go, before? When we
  were dancing." / "On your own? At night?" … "What for?" / "It's a cave," … "It's always open." "Maira" in her mouth: 1.
- **Chaos on the page (subjects, never phrases):** catastrophizing over the sandals; drinking the whole skin "as long as
  I could"; the sister-in-law who has "never been to Kirrha".
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the woman who expects every borrowed thing to be ruined and herself to be blamed, against
  Kallia, who expects only to have to carry it.
- **Sample line (calibration only; replaced 2026-10-19 because "lentils" as a joke is SPENT):** "You'll have it back by the
  feast, won't you? If it's late she'll say I sold it."
- **Ch.10 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish, hook-craft, the
  disruptor, continuity repair, polish cycle 1 and the PATH fix; every quote is on the page, checked by script):** "Somebody came
  in at the gate at a run. It was Nikaso, from along the lane, with flour on her hands and her black hair half out of its pins."
  (no gate count: polish cycle 1 cut the writer's gate count to keep ENTITY CH10-5 consistent with Ch.8's "Midway along the lane").
  "Kallia! That boy from Elis is at the lions telling everybody he brought you something written, from as far as Syria. Is it
  him? Is he living?" / "The gods be thanked." and her chaos on the page: "Nikaso put both hands over her mouth, and when she took
  them away there was flour on her lips." / her axis, the worry answered herself: "He'll be home for the god's day, won't he? And
  your sieve still at my house with the hole in it. Who'll he hear that from first? His mother. And then it's me that put a hole
  in his mother's sieve." / "Me? He remembers me?" … "He used to drop snails in my water jar." (she looks at the papyrus "as if it
  might say so again", the chapter's one "as if") / one speech, no look between its halves (the disruptor cut the look): "A
  child. And you out here spinning, with a grandson in Syria. I'd have been in the lane with it already, telling every woman with
  a jar." (her joy runs ahead of the facts: she assumes a boy) / "I'll stand him two," and the news goes down the lane gate by
  gate: "Syria, and a wife, and a child." She takes the good news as good news (the chapter's public face of it). The sieve is now
  CLOSED as her topic; "Maira" in her mouth: 0 in Ch.10.

#### SOSTRATA — the baker's wife (the Ch.7 bath walk-on, named in Ch.8)
- **Attention / worldview:** bodies and household feuds, above all her husband's mother; she turns a grievance into a
  joke and a joke into a song.
- **Vocabulary band:** loud, earthy town Greek; bread, dough, ovens, beds, mothers-in-law; bawdy without one obscene word.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** running commentary told to the whole room ("she said to anyone who would listen", her one
  group-address tag); put-downs with a name in them ("Your husband says plenty, Timo, for a man who's been married since
  the vintage."; "Not a man in this town will climb that stair with a stone for your feet, Timo, not if the god himself
  sent for him.").
- **Rhythm:** a complaint, a put-down, a verse.
- **Evasion — never says:** anything tender about the baker (twenty years) except by insult.
- **Physicality / silence:** soft and heavy; in Ch.7 a red clay lamp in her fist; in Ch.8 she hauls herself onto the cliff
  edge on all fours, goes by the dance "at a heavy trot", has her feet in Timo's lap, beats her own knees with both fists
  at the joke, and gets out of her frozen cloak and stands it on its hem in the snow.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (bodies, feuds) and register (bawdy song). The baker's song and its
  refrain are SPENT after Ch.8.
- **Bleed guard:** must not take Nikaso's dread or Kallia's labor-reckoning; she never asks a practical question. No
  maxims in her mouth (dialogue-polish cut a maxim about mothers-in-law from her whisper); her advice is her own grievance.
- **Ch.8 as written (SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** "I've
  left his mother the dough," … "and if she lets it rise past the rim, I'll smell it from here. She'll do it to spite me.
  She'll stand over it and watch it go." / "Tell him nothing; that's how mine's lasted twenty years." / after Philista's
  rebuke, lower: "He comes to whoever's awake," / "Stamp," … "Harder. I've his mother under each foot, and look how warm I
  am." / "You'll cook them," / "You're smoking," … "It's not the same thing." / "Some child's hopping home barefoot for
  want of this." / "There," … "It can keep the wind off you while I thaw." / "The baker will be asleep on the flour sacks,"
  … "with his mother's dough climbing out of its pan beside him, and the two of them snoring at each other." / "Not even a
  dead one?" / "Well," … "she can have the loan of ours." / the song and its verses ("Just the one,"; "Philista's man went
  off to the Athenians—"; "The Syracusan's man stayed home in Syracuse," … "and he—") / the whisper: "Give her the stone,
  before he can, and say it was your idea. The baker's mother let me off a whole burnt batch for one, the first winter." /
  "Go to sleep." Talks in her sleep, "a word of it about the dough".
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the woman who makes a song of her grievance, against Nikaso, who makes a dread of hers.
- **Sample line (calibration only):** "Twenty years and he still salts my bread before he's tasted it."

#### PHILISTA — leader of the Delphian Thyiades; the drum on her hip (Ch.8)
- **Attention / worldview:** the rite done properly, and the Athenian women who come every other winter and do it wrong.
  Her grudge is Athens.
- **Vocabulary band:** commands to the whole company; the god's household ("Your brother's gone away for the winter and
  left you the house"), never theology.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** a bark to the group, then a dry grudge or pride in the rite ("Husbands," … "On my cliff, and the
  god not woken."; "Put it straight. I bring my women down crowned."; "Any woman who sleeps by herself tonight wakes up
  dead, and I've never lost one yet.").
- **Rhythm:** short orders; one grudging aside.
- **Evasion — never says:** her own man except as a verdict ("He did," … "And they can keep him.").
- **Physicality / silence:** lean, old and upright, hood back, drum on her hip, strikes it once with the butt of her
  palm; inspects each woman at the head of the cliff; beats the drum "flat and hard" to call the women in; goes round the
  pen on her knees pushing them together.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (the rite and the Athenians) and syntax (orders, then a grudge or
  pride). **WATCH: the doubled bark** (an order repeated inside one speech: the draft had "Louder… Louder!" and "Down!…
  and down"; cycle 1 cut both). On the page now: only the ritual "Wake up, little one. … Wake up and have it!". Hold it to
  **1 per chapter**, and never as her way of speaking.
- **Bleed guard:** not Sosandros's correct forms (his are clerical; hers are rivalry); not Kallia's labor axis: no jokes
  or threats about who carries what in her mouth (dialogue-polish moved "carry" to Kallia; her second clause is grudge or
  pride, never carrying).
- **Ch.8 as written (SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** "No
  Athenians," … "The roads are shut, they say. They say it whenever it suits them. For once he'll be woken properly, and
  nobody from Athens standing at my elbow to tell me how it's done in Athens." / "Husbands," … "On my cliff, and the god
  not woken." / "He's had a good autumn." / "Wake up, little one. Your brother's gone away for the winter and left you the
  house. Wake up and have it!" / "He's been asleep three months," … "Louder!" / "Your ivy's slipped," … "Put it straight. I
  bring my women down crowned." / "Torches lit, all of you, and down, before it shuts the stair." / "No man comes up here,"
  … "Not tonight, nor any night." … "We stay. Back to the pen. Get under its wall, and bring every stick that will burn." /
  "An Athenian," / "He's woken," … "He's had his dance and his wine, and he's gone back into his basket to sleep it off. The
  rest of the night's ours. Say what you like." / "He did," … "And they can keep him." / "Close," … "All of you, with your
  backs to the wall, and your cloaks over the lot." / "He can't hear you from Delphi. We can." (no "girl" since cycle 1).
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only one who measures everything against Athens, against Sosandros, who measures it
  against the form.
- **Sample line (calibration only):** "Two to a torch and keep your hems out of the fire; we're not Athenians."

#### TIMO — the bride; married at the vintage to a potter's son from the lower town (Ch.8)
- **Attention / worldview:** her new husband, and what his mother will do to her; everything she sees is a question
  about husbands (the husband test is hers: "And yours, Maira? Did he mind you coming?").
- **Vocabulary band:** very young town Greek; "my husband says…".
- **Syntax fingerprint:** reportage of her husband in one breathless run, then a promise she believes ("He promised.").
  Keep her runs to a husband's doings, never system talk (Fenn's "and"-chains are English and about systems).
- **Rhythm:** eager rush; a scream; a whisper.
- **Evasion — never says:** that she is afraid of his mother, except in a whisper in the dark ("And if his mother's in the
  house when I get back?" / "And the second?").
- **Physicality / silence:** ivy crown down over one eyebrow; a little flask of hair oil warm in her bosom (surrendered
  silently for the sandal); her hem smolders; two sandals that both bend the same way; throws a sandal that is somebody
  else's; lets Sostrata's feet stay in her lap.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (her husband) and syntax (breathless report, then a promise).
- **Bleed guard:** she does not tease for sport (Sostrata does); she asks because she wants to know.
- **Ch.8 as written (SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):**
  "Sostrata, is it true he comes to the new brides first?" … "My husband says he does. He says I'm to tell him everything
  after." / "Take your feet off me," … "Get them away. They're like fish." / "I'm burning!" / "It's off my mother," … "and
  she'll want it back." / "How should I know? It was next to me." … "It's got a patch on the strap. Who has a patch on her
  strap?" / "I'll go first," and the potter's son who "cried in the doorway", would "walk behind with his eyes shut", and
  is "to wait at the top of the street when it's light, with a hot stone in a cloth for my feet. He promised." / "Mine
  will." / "And yours, Maira? Did he mind you coming?" / "No husband?" … "At your age?" / "Was he?" … "Truly? That small?" /
  "Again," … "From the oven. Give her a verse of her own." Says her husband's name under her breath in the dark until
  Philista loses patience. "Maira" in her mouth: 1.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only one who still believes a man will climb the stair with a hot stone, against
  Sostrata, who has twenty years of proof he will not.
- **Sample line (calibration only):** "He's carving me a bird for the shelf, he says, and he won't let me see it."

#### EUTHYDIKOS — priest of Apollo at Delphi; a witness on Aphrodisia's manumission; hires Mara to mend his grandfather's incense stand (Ch.9)
- **Attention / worldview:** status and appearances: his grandfather's stand back in its place "for the god's return", his own
  name in stone (the letter-cutter: "His name's at the foot of half this wall; he likes it in stone."), and his chilblains.
  Money is weighed against who earns it, and he prices her by her material, never naming her sex: "A smith's price is for a
  smith's fire. I have wax from my own bees." (the bee-man's wax then becomes the fill, unpointed).
- **Vocabulary band:** courteous temple Greek; cult and fee words (the pelanos, the god's treasury, the archive, a stater); no
  oaths, no maxims.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** **speaks of Mara in the third person to his slave while she stands in front of him** ("Ask her what
  she means by it," / "What would she do instead?" / "And for this rod and this wax, what does she want?"), and turns to her
  only for a ruling; then short verdicts ("Half." / "In what light?" / "Daylight," / "The smith's price.").
- **Rhythm:** one long courteous period, then a one- or two-word ruling.
- **Evasion — never says:** a price first (the smith and Mara must name theirs); thanks; that the work is good (he pays "The
  smith's price" and gives her the next job instead).
- **Physicality / silence:** a long gray beard "combed out and cut square at its end, so neat that it looked put on over his
  own"; hands kept up his himation sleeves, "red and swollen across every knuckle with chilblains, tight and glossy as
  cherries"; purse on a cord round his neck. **Never touches Mara:** lets the coins fall "from a little above, so that his fingers stayed
  clear of my palm", then wipes his fingers on the bee-man's linen, "each one, the thumb last" (SPENT).
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by syntax (talking past her to the slave) and attention (status, his own name)
- **Bleed guard:** must not notice workmanship or materials (he cannot find the seam even with a lamp in a sunlit court); no
  form-questions in order (Sosandros's); no labor reckoning (Kallia's); no proverbs. **His fee itemization ("First the
  pelanos… Then the goat… And a Delphian… And a man…") is spent in Ch.9**: if he returns he never lists again.
- **Chaos on the page (subjects, never phrases):** the chilblains ("In Syracuse, what do they do for chilblains?" / Mara:
  "Wool on the hands," … "With a place for each finger." / "Like a slave at the olive picking."); the lamp fetched into a court
  full of sun.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only speaker who talks about Mara to somebody else while she stands in front of him, against
  Sosandros, who asks her the form's questions directly without looking up.
- **Sample line (calibration only):** "Tell the woman she may stand in the shade while I decide; the sun is the god's, not hers."
- **Ch.9 as written (Writer, 2026-10-19. SYNCED to the final page 2026-10-19, after dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity
  repair, polish cycle 1 and the bending fix with finalize lines; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** "Ask
  her what she means by it," the priest said to the slave (his one "Ask her"; dialogue-polish cut the second) / "Yours, is it?"
  / "And if the bowl had dropped into your fire this morning?" / "What would she do instead?" / "And for this rod and this wax,
  what does she want?" / the narration's turn: "Up to then Euthydikos had looked at the slave, or at the smith, or at the air a
  little behind my shoulder, where a husband might have stood. This question he asked of me." / "Where does a woman learn
  bronze?" / "He sought the answer from the slave, and the slave had none for him, having no beans left to examine." / the price
  of the god, one unbroken speech: "For a Syracusan? First the pelanos, into the god's treasury, before he'll hear you at all.
  What a Syracusan pays I'd have to send to the archive to learn, to find whether your city ever made terms with the god, and if
  it did, whether anybody in it remembers. Call it a stater." / "Then the goat. Cold water goes on its head at the altar, and it
  must tremble from the hooves up, every leg of it, or the god isn't receiving that day; it goes on the fire whatever it did, and
  home you go, and back you come next month with another goat." / "And a Delphian to stand for you, since you aren't one, and
  he'll expect a present for his trouble. And a man to carry the thing in, since you aren't that either." / to Mara's "I know
  about the man.": "The rest is only money." / "A stater. For wax." / "Your price?" / "And three more for the bowl, when it fell
  in your fire." / "A smith's price is for a smith's fire. I have wax from my own bees." / "Half." / "In what light?" / "Pay the
  smith for his clay," he told the slave, and then, from the house door, "In Syracuse, what do they do for chilblains?" (no turn
  at the door) / "Like a slave at the olive picking." / "Daylight," he said, from inside. / at the inspection he "circled the
  stand before he bent to it", sends for a lamp "though the court was full of sun", and "He put his face so close to it that his
  beard brushed the bowl"; "His lips were moving." / "Here?" / "Show me." / "The smith's price," / the coins "from a little
  above", the linen, "each one, the thumb last" / "There's that smith's lead in the top that wants cutting out," … "When I
  send, come back and do the bowl." He is a named witness on Aphrodisia's manumission (the document); the page never points at
  it. "Syracuse" as a Delphian's custom question is at 2 across two chapters (Ch.8 Nikaso, Ch.9 his chilblains): no third.

#### PRAXIAS — Kallia's cousin on her mother's side; small olive grower on the Kirrha road below Delphi, one wall from Valens's yard; keeps a fighting quail (Ch.11; Valens proposes him as the Delphian who stands "at the door", Ch.13)
- **Attention / worldview:** money and odds: what a thing costs, what a stranger can be made to pay, which bird will win at the
  ring behind the stadium. Everything has a price, and family is a discount he advertises.
- **Vocabulary band:** Phocian town Greek; money words (drachmas, obols, "for family"); the quail ring. No oaths, no proverbs.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** names his price first, then the reason it is generous ("Five drachmas," … "That's for family. A stranger
  I'd charge eight, and he'd pay it, because who else has he got?"), then bargains down against himself ("Four," … "For family, four,
  and the standing thrown in." / "Three and a half, then. I'm robbing my own children.").
- **Rhythm:** easy, unhurried sales talk, never loud ("He was not shouting." / "He doesn't have to.").
- **Evasion — never says:** anything about the notice or a Thracian; that he needs the money (the quail ring says it for him).
- **Physicality / silence:** "a well-fed, slow-spoken, early-rising man who kept his beard clipped to a fringe" (continuity W-13; the draft's "soft, heavy" was Ch.7's); the quail in the crook of his
  arm, "its eyes nearly closed", its back smoothed "with a fingertip, from the neck down, all the while he talked"; stands in a
  gateway "until he had priced the place". Leaves "telling the quail about it".
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (prices and odds) and syntax (the price first, then the favor it supposedly is)
- **Bleed guard:** not Kallia (labor; never talks money about herself); not the Ch.9 goat man (who prices by the animal); not
  Euthydikos (who never names a price first: Praxias always does). "little cousin" is at 2 on the page: CAP REACHED, vary or drop;
  "for family" at 2: vary.
- **Chaos on the page:** the same morning he fails to sell Mara his standing he loses two obols on "the Amphissan's new bird against
  the baker's" and calls the baker a thief (Felix's report).
- **The target of Valens's mind-reading:** Valens is sure he has gone to inform ("He looked at the string. He looked at the house
  twice." / "He turned uphill. Not to his own door."); he went to the wine-seller and the quails. Harmless; money only.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only speaker who names his price before anyone asks and then lowers it himself, against
  Euthydikos, who makes everybody else name theirs.
- **Ch.11 as written (SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** "Three is a goat. Or it's the god's cake, if
  the priest's in a good temper. It isn't a man on his feet in the god's own house with all Phocis behind him, saying a Syracusan
  girl's question out loud." / "They laugh at any man that carries for a woman. I'd be hearing it at the ring till the vintage." /
  "For Kallia I'd do it for nothing. Kallia never asks for herself." / "You're the one asking." (with "real kindness, as if he were
  sorry about my arithmetic": the chapter's one "as if") / "By then you'll have sailed for Syracuse, little cousin, and I'll be here
  with my palm open." / "There's the Corinthian," … "He'll carry anything for anybody. Wood, oil, a priest's mother-in-law. He'd put
  your question in a pannier and bring the answer back up with your change." / "His mules are. Five, little cousin. Think it over
  while you fill your jars." / to Felix, "who liked him at once": "It did not," / "The god sits every month now till the winter. Go
  to him next month, when you've four." / to Valens: "Mule-man," … "That Syracusan of yours is a hard woman." He calls Valens "the
  Corinthian" and "Mule-man"; never Lupus.
- **Sample line (calibration only):** "Six to a stranger, four to you, and a cup at the plane tree after, which is money out of my
  own pocket."
- **Ch.13 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20; SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-20, polish cycle 1 + FZ1–FZ3; every quote below is on the page,
  checked by script):** paid at the terrace wall "out of the rag, in coins from the ford, into his hand" (no sum). "He weighed the silver in
  his palm and found it enough. He glanced at what the road had done to Valens, and back at the coins". Without the quail, "his left arm
  kept the crook of it anyway; now and then his fingers went over nothing there, smoothing." Odds, not prices: "Even money she stands like a
  post," … "They do, in a frost. A frost-morning goat will take a spring's worth on her head and think about her dinner." / "Next month. And
  then you pay again, Syracusan," … "and liked the sound of that". **"little cousin" stays at 2 (Ch.11 only); Ch.13 has 0, and there is no
  stop-himself beat.** After the drench, "Below the steps Praxias was telling the nearest man that he had said so." At the door he gives his
  name "twice, the second for the Thessalians' benefit": **"Praxias son of Kleon, of Delphi, for the house of Kallia."** (his father
  **Kleon**, mention only). Waits in the porch for the answer "for the quail ring; there's men there lay money on what the god tells
  strangers" and is refused it. Exit with no gait: "Praxias had gone off up past the theater toward the stadium and the quail ring with his
  wreath still on."


---

#### MNASON — herdsman on the high pasture above the Corycian Cave; summers his ewes in a hollow there; lets Valens the spare hut for the grazing (Ch.15)
- **Attention / worldview:** his ewes, his cheese, and how his dead mother did things. The mountain is the one place nobody counts
  what he eats. He keeps grudges against animals by name of deed (the torn-eared ewe "kicked me at the shearing").
- **Vocabulary band:** plain mountain Phocian; farm nouns (fig, kid, pail, gap, shearing, gorge). No town words.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** a complaint that runs on and lands on a grievance ("and you'll know whose doing that was"); a fronted object
  when he is moved ("Fig, she turned it with, every day she lived"); short orders over the gap ("Talk to her," / "Not her.").
  **Rule (Ch.15 eval C4): a fronted object in speech belongs to Mnason alone.** No other speaker fronts an object (Valens's
  "Neapolis" line was recast to "I've heard of Neapolis."), and Mnason uses it only when moved, about his mother: one per appearance
  at most. Narrated fronted objects are a separate count, CLOSED at 2 (see the at-risk block after Ch.15).
- **Rhythm:** talks to the pot, the ewes and Mara in the same voice except when it is about his mother.
- **Evasion — never says:** anything that asks who Mara and Valens are. He assumes ("your man") and tells, never asks.
- **Physicality / silence:** a goatskin cap with the ears still on it; sits at the fold's gap on a slab of rock and milks each ewe
  from behind. His age is Mara's estimate, spoken to Valens, not a narrated fact: "He is sixty if he is a day," … "and I have seen him
  walk into his own fold in the dark, looking for the gap." (The knife wiped on the thigh is used ONCE on the Ch.15 page, at the pot;
  do not repeat it as his business. The cap over his face for the noon sleep is once too.)
- **His huts and his bed (property facts, Ch.15):** he lives in **his own hut** by the fold, with the great pot outside it ("the
  great pot outside his own hut"; the kid's stomach is kept in there). The **second hut**, lent to Valens for the grazing, is his
  too, and so is everything in it: "Mnason's rush baskets hung in a row from pegs, and the smell of whey was in the stones."; **he
  made the bed** ("At the back he had made a bed by driving four forked stakes into the earth floor and laying poles across the
  forks, with cut bracken heaped on the poles and three fleeces on the bracken"); Mara slept in it all summer ("all summer it had held
  me, and I had never looked underneath it"). The hut is "dry stone shoulder-high, roofed with fir poles under brush and turves".
  The woodstack is his as well ("our woodstack, which in fairness was his"). **The bed is broken in Ch.15** (the fork at the head
  "split along its grain"; left "as it had fallen") and he has not seen it yet: "The herdsman will want his bed." / "He can have the
  end that's standing." Any later Mnason scene (none is planned) meets a broken bed, not a mended one.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by worldview (ewes, cheese, his mother's way) and syntax (the complaint that lands on a grievance)
- **Bleed guard:** must not notice materials or joins (Mara's axis) or read ground and exits (Valens's). No "Then…" retorts (Kallia's,
  capped). No proverb.
- **Chaos on the page (subjects, never phrases):** wants one twig off Valens's potted fig to turn his milk as his mother did, is refused,
  uses the kid's stomach he had all along, and grieves over the taste; the grudge ewe; the son's wife from Amphissa.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only voice that measures everything against a dead mother's kitchen, against Kallia, who measures it in
  labor, and Theano, who measures it in her own knees.
- **Ch.15 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20. SYNCED 2026-10-20: FINAL page, polish cycle 1 + the CH15-1 spring line + Z1–Z6; every quote
  below is on the page, checked by script):**
  - **Lines (each once):**
    - at the fold: "Talk to her," … "Talk, and she lets down. Sing, if there's nothing in your head. It makes no odds what, so it isn't
      sad. Sad, she holds it back." / "Not her." … "She kicked me at the shearing. You have her.";
    - at the pot: "That boy of yours sleeps through anything," … "A wolf could walk off with him, and your man would be up at those
      mules with his back to it. I've said so. Nobody listens to me up here but ewes." / "Wants turning.";
    - the fig twig: "A twig," … "Off the end, there, where it'll grow again. My mother turned her milk with fig, and hers did." /
      "There's no fig nearer than the gorge, and that's half a morning down and the whole of one back up." / "One twig." … "You'd not
      miss it."; then the rennet, in "a voice I had not heard him use to the ewes": "My mother never did it so. Fig, she turned it
      with, every day she lived, and it set as sweet as a girl. This'll taste of kid. You'll put it in your mouth and taste the kid in
      it, and you'll know whose doing that was.";
    - the juniper fight: "When you've beaten that," … "you can fight my cheese." / "That was a good drainer till you had it,";
    - supper: "Kid," he said, chewing. "Right through it." / "Green fig'll strip your lips," … "My brother ate six once, at the
      vintage, for a bet." / "He won the bet," / "Once. It was full of Corinthians." / "To my son's, at the foot of the gorge, where his
      wife can count every mouthful into me. Six months of her, and then the snow goes and I come up here to the ewes, who don't count."
      / "My son's wife is from Amphissa." / "Your man's shaved," (Valens: "He's called me that since our first night.");
    - the dog, out of the dark: "I'll have your hide for a pail,".
  - **Acts:** fetches the dried kid's stomach "on a string" (he had rennet all along); measures the twig "a finger's length, with his
    thumbnail"; brings the cheese over at sundown "in its rush basket, with the whey still weeping out of the weave onto his wrist";
    cuts it into four "with the knife he had meant for the fig"; "The cheese was good and Mnason said it was not and ate most of it."
    (the chapter's ugly sentence); "He licked his thumb."; leaves "to his ewes on that, and the answering was left to Valens."
  - **Valens's mind-reading (chaos) is aimed at him:** "He'll be over those stones some night." Mara answers it with his age and the
    gap (see Physicality). He knows nothing of the truth, the door or Pompeii, and never asks.
  - **Spent:** the fig-twig standoff; the rennet speech ("Fig, she turned it with" is the chapter's one spoken fronted object);
    "Your man's shaved,"; "Kid," … "Right through it."; the brother's six figs; "full of Corinthians"; "That was a good drainer till
    you had it,"; "I'll have your hide for a pail,"; "sleeps through anything"; the ewes "who don't count".
- **Sample line:** "That one's never stood still for a stranger, and she won't start for you." (Calibration only.)

#### ONESIMUS — the gray-haired clerk who sits the proconsul's record office door through the games, Corinth; a freedman clerk of the office (Ch.17)
- **Attention / worldview:** his own day: the door he is stuck minding, his food, his daughter's boy running in the boys' race while he
  cannot watch. Paperwork is labor, and labor is to be avoided. He takes Celer's money and Plautia's silver with the same face.
- **Standing (continuity I-10):** the page never names his patron. He has a daughter, a grandson, a lane and a wife who will sell
  Plautia's spoons in the market, so he is a local man of the office, not a freedman a one-year proconsul brought with him. Plautia's
  Ch.16 "the proconsul's freedmen" is her general word for the office's men and needs no correction.
- **Vocabulary band:** plain office Greek and Latin; "Lady." as a whole reply; documents read aloud flat, between mouthfuls.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** short, level statements; said to the fig cake once on the page ("His answer went to the cake."), and never
  again; answers with the least that will do ("A clerk's."); his one long speech is a grievance about his own family.
- **Rhythm:** slow; a fig between clauses.
- **Evasion — never says:** what he was paid to hide, or by whom; what he saw at the back door.
- **Physicality / silence:** "He dug them out singly with the blunt end of his stylus." (the figs from a pressed fig cake), and at the
  locked door "with his thumb"; a cobweb across his hair from the ladder ("He had the cobweb still across his hair."); "The clerk's
  sandals were old, one strap mended in a different leather"; buys "Two of the round ones" at the baker's oven each morning, "a coin in
  his fingers ready for the baker"; "He found a seed in a back tooth with his tongue."; "locked the door with a key on a thong, and
  tried it." The figs are eating, never a fidget: no handling beats, and no later walk-on eats through a scene the same way.
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (his own grievances) and physicality (eating through everything)
- **Bleed guard:** never Mara's eye for materials; never Myrrhine's prices (the dowry figure is read off the board, unquoted: "He named
  a figure and ate a fig."); never Celer's warmth; never Kallia's labor-cost.
- **Chaos on the page:** wants his grandson to LOSE, in one speech: "My grandson runs with the boys today. My daughter's boy. Twelve.
  He'll have run by now." … "I'd have liked to see him lose. His father thinks he'll run at Olympia. He's told the whole lane. If the
  boy loses today there'll be peace in my daughter's house till the grapes are in."
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only man in the book who saves anybody out of sheer unwillingness to write it down.
- **Ch.17 as written (Writer, 2026-10-21. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21: dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity repair, polish
  cycle 1 + F1–F14; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** he is **the gray-haired clerk of Ch.16** (ENTITY
  record-office-clerk-gray; CH16-10 resolved by the page: "The gray one, then. We had wanted the other."); the young drinker does not
  appear, his table does: "The second table, across the room, stood bare, its stool pushed in and a wine ring dried on the wood." (the
  Ch.16 plan's words are not reused). **Naming:** "the clerk" in narration until Celer names him ("We'd have done that, wouldn't we,
  Onesimus?"), then "Onesimus" only: "Onesimus said, his mouth full of bread"; "Onesimus took another bite of his bread."; "Onesimus
  finished his bread, fetched his cake from inside, locked the door with a key on a thong, and tried it." **Lines, in order:** "When
  the governor came back," … "the colony's old books were carted over here, and nobody's sorted them since." / "They're in this
  office." … "The old years went up the ladder." / "There's no one but me in today to mind the door." / "The boys race out on the
  Isthmus this morning. There's not a soul left in Corinth to come through it." / "The year?" / "Lady." / "Which year did you say?" /
  "There's three books of it." / "Highest shelf in the place. The whole run of them." / "It'll be the middle one. Dowries are the
  middle of a year. Everybody marries in the spring." / "That'll be the last." / "You read, lady." / the dowry read aloud ("*Plautia,
  daughter of Sextus*" … "*Done at Corinth*") / "A clerk's." / "Your father's. The freedman's had a chip out of the rim." / "Now?" /
  "The lane door," … "is by the oven, if you'd sooner not be kept. He'll keep you till noon." / at the back door: "That's out of the
  emperor's year," / "Two of the round ones." / "He's not opened it," … "He'll not open it. He has a copyist who'll keep it under his
  bed till everybody's forgotten it. He's paid the copyist a year ahead." / "You'd want to be gone before somebody decides to remember
  it." / "I'd not know about that." / the grievance (above) / in the colonnade: "Lady didn't ask," / "Legate." / "It'll be over." /
  "Not today, legate." (no receipt, no seal, no writing) / "If anybody asks me tonight," … "the book left here for the copyist, and I
  was at the Isthmus by noon. You were never in." / "My wife will have them on a cloth in the market by the Kalends." / "If you hear
  how the boys came in, send me word." / "Gods hear you." **"lady" in his mouth on the final page:** "You read, lady." only, plus the
  whole-line "Lady." and "Lady didn't ask,". **Acts:** "His hand found the wrong year's hole at the first try; he drew the book from it
  and clamped it to his side, spine inward."; "He eyed us under his scaffold like a bench somebody had left in his way."; "He left
  under the columns, by the eastern road." He hid the book in the wrong year's slot, told the brother's freedman it was "out with a
  copyist" (Ch.16), and hands it to Celer unopened. **Spent:** all of the above, including "His answer went to the cake.", "Highest
  shelf in the place.", "It'll be over." and "That'll be the last."; he does not return unless the plot needs Corinth.
- **Sample line:** "If it isn't in the book, lady, it was never in the book, and I'm not writing that it wasn't." (Calibration only.)

#### MYRRHINE — Plautia's maid, from her father's house; Greek-speaking; follows Latin when it is slow (Ch.16; backfill Ch.17)
- **Attention / worldview:** the household's things and what they fetch: she knows where every one of Plautia's given-away possessions
  went and what it costs to buy it back. Loyal by thrift, not by sentiment.
- **Vocabulary band:** Corinthian household Greek; prices, cloaks, shoes, flasks, litters, hems. Follows Latin when it is slow ("She
  had caught the cloak and the cook, and no more.", once, at Plautia's quick Latin about the cook).
- **Syntax fingerprint:** a price, then the reason it is a fair one ("I'll give you a drachma for it, which is a good price for a thing you
  never paid for."); objections as a string of small practical failings ("She doesn't know where the flask goes. She doesn't know which
  side you get down from a litter. She'll stand on your hem.").
- **Rhythm:** silent at her lupins until something of her mistress's is at stake, then quick.
- **Evasion — never says:** why her mistress gives things away, or why she weeps; she states the habit and goes back to her stool. (The
  disruptor cut the narrator's gloss on the habit; her two speeches run as one and the habit explains itself.)
- **Physicality / silence:** shells lupins into her skirt and "shook them onto the coals, and the room filled with the smell of their
  burning"; when overruled, "split her next lupin so hard that half of it shot under the chest"; "eyed the bracelet without touching
  it"; carries "the bundle of towels and the oil flask on its strap" to the baths. (Lupins are her food, once per scene at most; never a
  fidget.) **On the final page the lupins are in two scenes only:** C1 (the shelling; the skins on the coals) and C4 (the split lupin).
  In C2 she "returned to her stool and watched the curtain"; C3 has none. (The maid holding the cloak on the stair in D3 is unnamed.)
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by worldview (what things fetch, and buying them back) and evasion (never explains her mistress)
- **Bleed guard:** must not notice how a thing is made (Mara's) or read labor's cost the way Kallia does (who carries it); hers is the
  resale price. No "Then…" retorts. No proverb. **Pricing is hers alone** (Felix asks the bracelet's weight, not its price).
- **Chaos on the page (subjects, never phrases):** buys back what Plautia gives away: "I had her mother's cloak back off a rope-maker's
  wife at the Saturnalia, and her good shoes off a girl at the baths. Two drachmas." ("a fuller's wife" belongs to Plautia's nursing
  line alone); jealous of Mara taking her place with the writing case.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only voice in the book that prices a gift for buying back, against Kallia, who prices it in labor, and
  Grata (Part Two), who prices everything for sale.
- **Ch.16 as written (Writer, 2026-10-20. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21: dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity repair, polish
  cycle 1 + F1–F8; every quote checked by script):** lines, in order: "He looks at it every time the chest is open," (C1, in Greek, of
  the cook and the cloak) / "A drachma," … "I'll give you a drachma for it, which is a good price for a thing you never paid for." /
  "Everything she gives away is for sale again by morning." / the cloak and the shoes / "Two drachmas." / "She knows her shoes are on
  her feet." (to Mara's "Does she know you buy them back?") / "I carry your case," / "She doesn't know where the flask goes. She
  doesn't know which side you get down from a litter. She'll stand on your hem." Plautia hands the list back ("You will show her where
  the flask goes, and which side, and she will stand off my hem."). **Spent:** the buy-back offer; the flask-and-litter objection; "She
  knows her shoes are on her feet."; "He looks at it every time the chest is open,". **Ch.17 rule:** no price figure in her mouth, and
  no lupins.
- **Ch.17 (backfill pieces only; Writer, 2026-10-21. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21: dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity
  repair, polish cycle 1 + F1–F14; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** before dawn she pressed a beaker of water on
  Mara ("because Myrrhine had pressed it on me"); "Myrrhine had packed the case three times over in the lamplight that morning, each
  time tighter"; "marched me to and fro behind an empty chair to cure me of her mistress's hem": "You're on it again. You'll have her
  over in the street. Further back. She gets down on the right, always, from a child; her nurse was left-handed, and nobody has ever
  told her otherwise." / "The knife is her grandfather's," / "Everything in this case comes home tonight. If it goes to anybody else, I
  shall know whose house." Mara wears her dress. Plautia gives the knife away anyway ("Myrrhine will find you," … "She finds
  everything."); Valens: "Her woman can come to Puteoli for it." (a dry line that binds no scene: Ch.18 is not bound to bring her).
  Mara keeps the knife in her belt. **Rules kept:** no price, no lupins. **Spent:** the hem drill; "You'll have her over in the
  street."; the left-handed nurse; "I shall know whose house".
- **Sample line:** "That brooch went to the lamp-seller's wife at the last market day, and I'll have it back by the Kalends for less than she
  paid in compliments." (Calibration only.)

#### PACUVIUS — master of the goose-stern merchantman out of Lechaion for Puteoli; an Italian, father of a newly married daughter, PACUVIA (Ch.18–19)
- **Attention / worldview:** what will be said about him at the other end. Every rule on his deck is about his daughter's new
  mother-in-law at Puteoli and the magistrate's people on the Puteoli quay; the fare is beside the point ("It isn't the fare.").
  Weather and the yard are his clock. He once carried a runaway wife and had her husband's brothers aboard at Ostia with cudgels, in
  front of his six-year-old girl, and he will not have it again.
- **Vocabulary band:** "quick and hard, a harbor Latin stuffed with sailors' Greek, and he stayed at the rail to use it on us" (R10;
  Mara's one description of it; never again); ship and trade nouns (plank, yard, mole, fare, marble, the Kalends, the Ides); no oaths on
  the page (the oath is the plank sailor's).
- **Syntax fingerprint:** delivers his conditions as long practical run-ons that stack the consequences ("and I'm taking her … and his
  mother will have …"), then a short flat verdict ("Then she's nobody's, which is worse."). He answers an argument by restating the
  terms. He states the law of marriage as a shipmaster states a harbor rule: no priest, no cake, two others to hear it.
- **Rhythm:** a long grievance, then one line; shouts only when the yard is up.
- **Evasion — never says:** that he would be sorry to leave them on the quay; anything warm to Valens or Mara; his daughter's name.
- **Physicality / silence:** one sandal; the other foot bare and very white, held clear of the boards and later set down "with care";
  the lost sandal is his own bad day (he asks the quay to watch the water for it). Stands at the stern rail under the goose's neck and
  never comes down the plank. "spread his hands" once; "squinted past the mole"; "scowled up at the yard"; reads Valens "with no
  great liking for the flour on him"; answers Mara by talking to Valens ("He spoke to Valens."; "To Valens he said").
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (respectability at the far port) and syntax (consequence run-ons, then a verdict)
- **Bleed guard:** never Mara's materials eye; never Valens's footing talk; never Celer's warmth; no counting or tallying device (his
  booking is said once as a booking, then varied: "a muleteer and a wife and a boy" / "A husband with his wife and their lad" / "A
  muleteer, then, with a wife and a boy").
- **Chaos on the page:** the lost sandal ("And if any of you sees a sandal floating past the mole, it's mine."). Spent; never again.
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only man in the book who marries two people to satisfy a woman neither of them will ever meet.
- **Ch.18 as written (Writer, 2026-10-21. SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21 (md5 `dbd5ac50…`): dialogue-polish, disruptor, continuity
  repair W-01…W-13, polish cycle 1 (C1–C4, R1–R13, D1–D4), finalize F1–F3; every quote below is on the page, checked by script):** named
  once, by the plank sailor ("Pacuvius, they're done."); "the master" in narration. **Lines:** "You'll be the muleteer," / "He paid me
  at sunrise, for a muleteer and a wife and a boy, and their mules, and a young fig with its roots in a jar." / "I have the boy, and I
  have the mules and the tree. Your boy's been on that plank since the third hour, telling the crew about a mountain. When they asked
  him, to pass the morning, how long his master had been married, he told them you aren't." (not sample-09's frame: continuity W-10) /
  "Every man on my deck heard him." / "It isn't the fare." / the daughter-and-mother-in-law speech ("My girl's aboard. She was married
  at the Kalends to a man at Puteoli she's never set eyes on"; "A husband with his wife and their lad, she'll swallow."), whose fastest
  stretch outruns Mara ("if somebody's..." / "The rest of it outran me."), then the Ostia brothers: "I carried a man's wife once. Her
  husband's brothers came aboard at Ostia with cudgels, and mine was six, and she saw it." / "Wife is what I was told, with a purse to
  go with it, and then he was away to the races before I'd weighed what was in it. Your boy told me otherwise for nothing." / "Not out
  of this harbor before the Ides, and that one's carrying marble, and she won't take a mule." / to Mara's registration, past her: "They
  don't keep Delphi's rolls at Puteoli." He spoke to Valens. "I'll put no man's wife aboard, nor any man's woman either." / "Then she's
  nobody's, which is worse." / "Marry her, then, if you mean to, and be quick. That one at the plank can stand for it, and the boy. It
  wants no priest and no cake, only the two of you saying so with two others to hear it, and I don't care to be told how they manage it
  in Corinth. You have till the yard's at the top." (no history of the rite: continuity W-05 cut his folk-history clause) / "Throw a nut
  at this ship and you'll swim after her." / "I heard." / to his daughter: "Yours had a cake," / "A muleteer, then, with a wife and a
  boy. As I was told at sunrise, and nobody on my ship has lied to anybody. Get them aboard." / the sandal: "He set the bare foot down
  on the boards with care." … "And if any of you sees a sandal floating past the mole, it's mine." / "The yard's up!" the master
  shouted. "Muleteer! Aboard or off the plank, the lot of you, and I'm not particular which!" **Possessives:** dialogue-polish cut his
  my-plus-ship-noun run from 12 to 3 (my deck, My girl, my ship), with "mine was six" and the sandal's "it's mine" besides. **Spent:**
  all of the above; the yard as a deadline; the no-priest-no-cake rule (stated once in the book); "They don't keep Delphi's rolls at
  Puteoli."; "I'll put no man's wife aboard, nor any man's woman either."; "Throw a nut at this ship and you'll swim after her."
- **Ch.19 (SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21 (md5 `41dedf4d…`): dialogue-polish, hook-craft, disruptor, continuity repair W-01…W-14, polish cycle 1 (L1–L2, C1–C4, R1–R15, D1–D6), finalize F1–F5; quotes checked by script):** his lines and acts at sea and on the Puteoli quay are in the Ch.19 walk-on note ("Pacuvius at Puteoli"). His
  daughter is named only in narration (continuity W-12; see PACUVIA below); he never says her name, so his evasion holds. Done.
- **Sample line:** "Rope's for the ship, the bucket's for the ship, and if your boy is sick he's sick over the side, not on my
  marble." (Calibration only.)

#### PACUVIA — Pacuvius's daughter; married at the Kalends to a man at Puteoli she has never seen, on her way to his mother's house (Ch.18 unnamed, "a girl in a blue hood"; named in Ch.19)
- **Attention / worldview:** the mother-in-law's verdict. Everything she does on the crossing is aimed at the woman waiting at the foot
  of the plank: the greeting rehearsed until the steersman knows it, the brooch that has to hold because her father says "she looks
  at everybody's brooch". She is a bride going to be read by a stranger, and she knows it.
- **Vocabulary band:** a Latin learned for one occasion ("in her careful Latin"; the set greeting); quay Greek that she turns out to
  have had all along ("and her Greek was suddenly as good as anybody's"). Household nouns: pin, wax, thorn, hood, her mother, her
  father.
- **Syntax fingerprint:** a practical worry run on to its consequence ("She'll look at my brooch before she looks at my face, my
  father says she looks at everybody's brooch, and if it won't hold I'll have to fasten myself up with a thorn."); then short and
  exact when the moment comes ("Married people," … "Corinthians. They had mules."). Her father's run-ons stack a ship's rules; hers
  worry at one object.
- **Rhythm:** quick and low to Mara; slow and formal for the greeting; two short sentences for the mother-in-law.
- **Evasion — never says:** that the brooch was ever broken ("Don't tell my father it was broken,"); anything about the husband she
  has not met.
- **Physicality / silence:** each beat once, never a habit. Ch.18: eats a cucumber from the end and drops "the bitter end over the rail
  into the harbor". Ch.19: sick "for every mile of the Straits"; at the mended brooch she "pinned her hood with the brooch, unfastened
  it, and pinned it again twice more to be sure." (SPENT: if she ever returns, her hands do something else); for the greeting, "her
  fingers laced in front of her".
- **Verbal tic:** none — distinguished by attention (the mother-in-law's verdict) and syntax (one worry run on to its consequence)
- **Bleed guard:** never Mara's eye for materials (Mara closes the coil; the girl knows only that "It goes in and it comes out
  again"); never her father's possessives or harbor rules; never Felix's stacked questions. No line of hers recurs, and nobody
  repeats her brooch line.
- **Chaos on the page:** checks the mended brooch over and over; "My mother had one that did that," … "She stopped it with wax." (the
  mother she is leaving, said to a stranger, once).
- **DISTINCTNESS GUARANTEE:** the only voice in the book that rehearses for one woman's eyes, against her father, who runs his deck for
  that same woman's tongue, and Felix, who performs for anyone watching.
- **Naming (continuity W-12):** Ch.18 labels her once ("The master's daughter") and otherwise "The girl in the blue hood" / "the girl";
  Ch.19 calls her "Pacuvius's girl" once, in the first paragraph, names her at "The master's girl, Pacuvia," and then "Pacuvia found
  me" and "Pacuvia went down our plank first, with the brooch at her throat,"; "the girl" after that. Only the narration names her.
- **Ch.18 (unnamed; the Ch.18 final page, md5 `dbd5ac50…`):** "At the rail amidships a girl in a blue hood was eating a cucumber from
  the end, and watching us over it."; her one line, as she "called along the deck": "Father," … "was mine like that?" (answered
  "Yours had a cake,"); "the girl considered that and dropped the bitter end over the rail into the harbor." The cucumber triggers
  Mara's memory; the memory is Mara's, never hers.
- **Ch.19 as written (SYNCED to the FINAL page 2026-10-21 (md5 `41dedf4d…`): dialogue-polish, hook-craft, disruptor, continuity repair W-01…W-14, polish cycle 1 (L1–L2, C1–C4, R1–R15, D1–D6), finalize F1–F5; quotes checked by script):** "Pacuvius's girl slept under an awning at the stern with her father across its opening on a folded
  sail."; "was sick for every mile of the Straits and well again by daylight" (L1: no Rhegium); every evening she "practiced the
  greeting she meant to give her husband's mother", which was "Mother of my husband, my father greets your house, and I enter it
  gladly," and she said it "in a whisper at first and then aloud, until the steersman could say it with her." (the chapter's one
  X-at-first-and-then-Y frame).
  **The brooch,** on the morning they made the bay ("She had on her good blue, and a brooch in her fist."): "It won't hold," … "The
  pin. It goes in and it comes out again. She'll look at my brooch before she looks at my face, my father says she looks at
  everybody's brooch, and if it won't hold I'll have to fasten myself up with a thorn." Mara closes the coil "with the girl's breath
  on my knuckles the whole while"; "My mother had one that did that," … "She stopped it with wax." / "She was already going aft." /
  "Don't tell my father it was broken," … "and was gone to show him." **The quay:** "Pacuvia went down our plank first, with the
  brooch at her throat, and her father behind her for once instead of at the rail."; "The girl said her greeting, the whole of it, in
  her careful Latin, with her fingers laced in front of her"; to the mother-in-law's "Those on your father's deck, who are they?":
  "Married people," the girl told her, "and her Greek was suddenly as good as anybody's", "Corinthians. They had mules." **Spent:**
  the brooch and its mending; the pinning; the greeting and its rehearsal; the wax; "Don't tell my father it was broken,"; "Married
  people," / "Corinthians. They had mules."; "her good blue". **Done unless she returns** (Puteoli in Ch.21–22, the letter board);
  if she does, she gets a fresh physical mark and new words.
- **Sample line:** "She'll have my girdle off me with her eyes before the soup, and if the knot's slack she'll say my mother never
  taught me." (Calibration only.)

## NO TICS (author's standing law, 2026-10-18; HARD; every book)

- **Tic-bearer roster: NONE.** No character in this book, **Mara included**, leads with a verbal tic,
  catchphrase, refrain, pet phrase, signature line or signature gesture. Every card above reads "Verbal
  tic: none — distinguished by <axis>". (`STATE.yaml` → `character_bible.tic_bearers: []`.)
- **No devices, for anyone.** Counting, numbering, listing, enumerating, pricing, rating, quantifying,
  cataloguing, condition-reporting and proverb-quoting are one device in different coats, and it is
  retired for everyone. **One-device-per-character map: empty.** Struck from the prior run: Fenn's
  numbering, Kallia's proverbs, Mara's catalogue-under-stress, the "bread-opinion," and Valens's
  fixed knot gesture.
- **Motifs are OBJECTS and SITUATIONS, never lines:** the watch, the film and its counter, the
  microcassette, the knucklebone, the Zippo, a hand at a threshold, the visible seam. Each is written in
  fresh words every time it recurs.
- **Chaos traits are subjects, not phrases.** Teeth, figs, the Phillies, onions, the automat and Mrs.
  Dunphy recur as topics, worded fresh, and never end a scene.
- **The allowlist holds three phrases, each capped at 3 uses book-wide:** "the one it brought" · "where
  the dead go out" · "the seventh of Bysios" (the Ch.13 title counts if it carries it). See
  `voice-dna.md` §4.
- **The Amelia Lesson.** In an earlier book a quantifying device and the phrase "never once" calcified
  into a tic and bled across the cast until everyone sounded alike. Earned tics spread too, which is why
  the budget is zero. **Watch the narrator's habits bleeding onto the cast.** Every card's bleed guard
  names the risk.
- **Enforced every chapter:** `voice_wear_check.py` (retired phrases, plus self-repetition of any 4–6-word
  phrase across 2+ chapters: BLOCKING); `tic_report.py --gate` (any non-allowlisted 3–6-word phrase 3+
  times book-wide in prose, or any 3+-word phrase 3+ times in dialogue: FAIL); `style_check.py`; the
  Evaluator's tic-proliferation scan (any tic caps Characters at 7.5); the Continuity Guardian's
  device-emergence check.

### Retired / at-risk phrase list
**Retired (fail on sight; seeded in `voice_wear_check.py`):**
- `never once`
- the **performed-candour family:** "I want that on the record," "I want this written down," "I want it
  noted," "I want to be exact," "I want to be clear," "I want to be honest," "I want to be precise," "let
  it be said (plainly)," "I will be honest," "I mention it because," "for the record," and, by the same
  rule, "truth be told," "if I'm honest," "to be fair," "I should say."
- the **foreshadowing forms** (`voice-dna.md` §0; `style_check --max-foreshadow 0`): "little did I know,"
  "I did not know yet," "I did not yet know," "I would (come to / learn / discover / understand /
  realize / find out)," "as I would later learn," "it would prove," "it would turn out," "the last time I
  would," "the last [X] I would," "for the last time," "I would never … again," "in (all) the years
  since," "over the years that followed," "the years to come," "the man I would marry / love / lose /
  become," "my future husband," "I never saw him again," "had I known," "even now," "I still remember,"
  "to this day," "looking back," "in hindsight."
- **At-risk after Ch.3 (2026-10-18):** a character turning to a DEVICE instead of Mara (a TV, a phone) —
  used twice (Szabo, the jeweler) and now closed; "My [part] went hot" (one per chapter at most; the
  crossing blush in Ch.3 is the only one there); "you could have [verb]" (spent); the watch "against my
  wristbone" (spent); "Then…" as a one-line pivot; paragraphs ending on a negation.
- **At-risk after Ch.1 (audit 2026-10-18; a third use anywhere in the book is a defect):** "Which one
  stops it?" (Emilio's recorded line — spent exactly twice in Ch.1, the anchor; never again); "the
  conservator in me"; "the [color] of [thing]" as a sentence shape; the item-by-item look; a character
  turning to a television/screen as a beat; "Then you…" as Szabo's reply shape. (Synced after Ch.7: the [color]-of-[thing]
  shape is CLOSED at 2 and SPENT; see the Ch.7 block.)
- **At-risk after Ch.5 (editor cycle 1, 2026-10-19; closed devices, a further use is a defect):** the
  **item-by-item look** (page count back to 2: Ch.1, Ch.3; CLOSED — any later look at a garment is one object
  or a duration, never a sequence); "**[color] going [color]**" (1, Ch.3: SPENT); "**laughed until [result]**"
  (1, Ch.3: SPENT; walk-on laughter gets no bodily aftermath); "**That one I had/heard whole**" and "hold on to
  it whole" (Ch.4 only: SPENT); the "**did not X; he Y**" pivot (SPENT); **"the size of a fingernail"** as a
  ruler (SPENT); **bread "full of grit"** (SPENT); the **sock "rolled tight" / "flat weight" / blind rummage
  past box and camera** (SPENT — Ch.19 and Ch.35 touches must be staged a third way); **the watch at her ear,
  "ticking, quick and…", its dead luminous paint** (none again until Ch.18); **the duffel sliding off her
  shoulder before a kneel** (SPENT); "**said to the [non-listener]**" (cap 1 per chapter; Ch.5 keeps only
  "said to the dark"); **"I said to his/her back"** (SPENT); **thumbnail probe** (max 1 per chapter);
  **knowledge-provenance aside** ("I knew X from [text/lab]": max 2 per chapter); **walk-on holding an
  object up to the eye** (max 1 per chapter; Ch.5's is the herald); **walk-on word-doubling** ("Salt them!
  Salt!", "Look. Look.", "Two days… Two days": none for walk-ons; the goat-boy's was his alone);
  **"the same… the same… the same"** triad (broken); **"the flat of her hand"** (1 per chapter);
  "**a [man/woman] of fifty**" introduction formula (1 per chapter); **"It was nearly dark"**, **"smelled of
  wet stone"**, **"side by side"** shoes (each spent at its earlier use); the **flashlight**: used once on the
  page in Ch.5 (the sheepfold, a dog goes frantic at the beam) and deliberately kept hidden in Kallia's
  storeroom — any later use must reckon with being seen.
- **At-risk after Ch.6 (Writer, 2026-10-19; closed or capped devices; extended 2026-10-19 from the dialogue-polish,
  disruption, continuity-repair and polish-cycle-1 logs and the Ch.6 eval):**
  - **Mara's long English speech to a listener who cannot follow it** (Ch.4 the goat-boy, Ch.5 Kallia at the
    spring, Ch.6 Valens on the cliff; Ch.5's content had repeated into Ch.6 until cycle 1): CLOSED, no fourth.
    Enforce it verbatim in the Ch.7–10 writer prompts.
  - **The watch wound backward** (the Ch.6 anchor): SPENT. Every later winding is forward and in fresh words.
  - **The "first Greek I understood/followed" move** (Ch.5, cut to one use; Ch.6 "I followed all of it without
    reaching"): SPENT book-wide.
  - **Valens naming himself as the traceable one** ("one man in Delphi who'd take a mule up Parnassus in the
    snow"): used once. It must not become his way of voicing fear. Later fear stays unspoken (his evasion) or goes
    into his attention (ground, exits, who saw).

  - **The command echoed as compliance** (an order, then the narrator repeating the order's word as her action):

    Ch.7 "Say it again." / "I said it again." (kept: the salt joke needs it). Cycle 1 cut Ch.7's other two (¶38, ¶158).

    each step") or not at all. The gate detector is not yet built (pipeline #15a), so this is enforced by hand.
  - **The "So I… / So she…" paragraph opener** (the narrator's consequence hinge): the gate (`tic_report.py`,
    `SO_OPENER_RX`) now caps it at **1 per chapter** and prints a WATCH at 4+ consecutive chapters. It has appeared in
    **six consecutive chapters** (Ch.2–6, one each on the page, plus the Ch.7 draft's two, at ¶34 and ¶132, both cut in
    cycle 1; the final Ch.7 has 0). **Ch.8: NONE**, so the streak stays broken. Open on the action itself.

    sits at the cap with the one it keeps, the father's name (¶334, "The Greek of it was out before I had decided
    anything about it."). Cycle 1 cut two more, and the related clause that had her eyes go down by themselves (¶376). Related forms book-wide: Ch.1
    "before I had checked anything at all", Ch.4 "without deciding to", Ch.6 "I had not meant to". **Ch.8: none**; let

    construction, ruled out by the evaluator, but hold it at those two.
  - **The memory-paragraph kicker** (a remembered scene closing on a short dry sentence that comments on it): Ch.3, Ch.5,
    Ch.6 ×2, and Ch.7's slides memory at ¶288, where the continuity repair wrote it and cycle 1 cut it. **SPENT.** A memory

    the bell and "said we could go". Every edit pass (repairs included) gets this pattern check; the gate heuristic is

  - **NEW NAMED RISK: whole set pieces rebuilt from the chapter just finished.** Ch.10's letter was built on Ch.9's curse tablet
    beat for beat (a "It was a [sheet/strip] of [material]" sentence with a ruler from Mara's hand; the fold count; the fastener;
    the water damage; the fastener "worked" out; the folds opened slowly with a material simile; the hand described by how its
    lines run), and Kallia's look at the name reused Ch.9's reading-time measure for Valens at the notice. Both held the floor in
    cycle 0. **Rule: before writing any object-handling or document scene (an object arriving, a document opened or read, a look at
    a text, an exit), list the previous chapter's set pieces and their beat order, and stage the new one in a different order with
    different beats.** The reading-time measure for a look (`the time it would have taken … read`, `while a slow man might read`) is
    capped at 1 book-wide and is used (Ch.9).

## CAST-WIDE DISTINCTNESS MATRIX (cover-the-name, pre-solved)

| Character | Sharpest distinguisher | Contrast vs nearest character |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mara | sees how things were made and mended; qualifies, compares, spirals | **Valens:** he never compares and never asks why of a feeling · **Fenn:** precise 1998 English against his runaway 1968 |
| Valens | no comparisons, no *why* of feelings; short level statements; maintenance hands | **Philokrates:** silence against kindly explanation · **Mara:** see above |
| Fenn | frozen-1968 slang in "and"-chains; systems before people; future tense about going back | **Mara:** see above · **Halloran:** slang and certainty against euphemism and forms · **Emilio:** names dates, against never naming one |
| Kallia | answers questions with tasks; attention to labor; never speaks of herself | **Theano:** speaks only of herself · **Grata:** few words against a flood |
| Theano | complains about her knees and the priests; no comfort; no explanation of the god or the hour (once, of the crows) | **Kallia:** see above |
| Felix | stacks questions; boasts; always moving | **Lucius:** one grave question, then waiting · **Grata:** asks against tells |
| Plautia | formal Latin periods broken by one blunt question; no contractions | **Celer:** geniality that never breaks; contractions and "we" |
| Celer | genial first-person plural; conditionals; never "wife," never a threat | **Plautia:** see above |
| Myrrhine | prices a gift for buying back; a string of small practical failings; never explains her mistress | **Kallia:** prices in labor · **Grata:** prices for sale |
| Grata | tells you about yourself; never asks; touches arms | **Felix:** asks · **Kallia:** terse · **Szabo:** sells by contempt |
| Lucius | grave, literal, whole sentences; acts on instructions | **Aelia:** grabs and exclaims · **Marcus:** taught manners against untaught gravity |
| Aelia | exclamations, imperatives, touch and taste | **Lucius:** see above |
| Emilio (tape) | warm, locates by *where*, never names *when* | **Fenn** and **Halloran:** see above |
| Halloran | euphemism for death; dates and deadlines; apology then request | **Emilio:** warmth without dates |
| Irene Szabo | no contractions; verdicts on authenticity; no condolence | **Grata:** flood and flattery |
| Philokrates | patient explanation in the sickroom "we"; no promises | **Valens:** explains nothing |
| Secundus | rehearsed civic grandeur collapsing into street Latin | **Grata:** never performs |
| Ariston (letter) | Greek letter formula; greets the neighbors, not the room | **Fenn's letters:** no formula, all voice |
| Marcus | taught formula, then a child's rush | **Lucius:** see above |
| Margaret Fenn | never quoted; absence | **Fenn:** fills every silence but hers |
| Mrs. Dunphy | silent, mutating memory | **Emilio:** a remembered voice |

**No two characters share a sharpest distinguisher. No character carries a tic or a device.**

### Cover-the-name test (run against the finished bible, 2026-10-18): **PASS**
Ten unattributed lines, one per frequent speaker, written fresh for the test and not for use on the page:

1. "Ground's bad past the bridge. We lead them."
2. "Is he fighting Tuesday? Is it the thraex? Can I carry his shield in? Who's paying?"
3. "Beans first. Then you can talk."
4. "And the thing is, the door was open in sixty-two and I was laid up in Misenum with a fever,
   three days, holy cow, three lousy days."
5. "We needn't make an enemy of each other over a little paper, need we?"
6. "You will forgive me for asking it plainly: did he ever love you?"
7. "You're tired, you've been tired since Saturnalia, you'll be wanting the lentils. Sit. Eat."
8. "My knees, girl. Ask your question before they give out."
9. "Look how they've riveted it; somebody cared about this pot long after it stopped being worth
   caring about."
10. "Tata, is the boat coming now or after?"

Key: 1 Valens (short, level, footing) · 2 Felix (stacked questions, arena) · 3 Kallia (task before
talk) · 4 Fenn (1968 idiom, "and"-chain, systems) · 5 Celer (genial "we," soft question) · 6 Plautia
(formal clause, then the blunt question) · 7 Grata (tells you about yourself, then commands) · 8 Theano
(her body, impatience) · 9 Mara (how a thing was made and mended) · 10 Lucius (one grave, literal
question). Every line is identifiable from the four axes alone, with no catchphrase involved. Each line
also uses a register no other card allows: Valens's line has no comparison, Felix's lines are all
questions, Kallia's are imperatives about labor, Fenn's is the only modern idiom, Celer's is the only
genial "we," Plautia's is the only formal-then-blunt construction, Grata's never asks, Theano's is the
only self-complaint, Mara's is the only line about workmanship, and Lucius's is the only child's grave
question. **The test passes.** If any future entry fails it, differentiate on the four axes before
finalizing the chapter. Never add a tic to fix it.
